| private scene A | `cam3` | `cam4`, `cam5` | 120 | 59,176 | 6.45%, 8.47% | −6.45% | 7.9 | 5.30%, 13.81% | −3.78% | 23.0 |
| private scene A | `cam4` | `cam1`, `cam3` | 120 | 70,344 | 3.86%, 5.67% | +3.86% | 3.9 | 5.27%, 13.07% | +5.19% | 19.0 |
| private scene A | `cam5` | `cam2`, `cam3` | 120 | 68,508 | 1.57%, 4.04% | +0.37% | 1.6 | 4.98%, 8.46% | +4.96% | 25.2 |
| private scene A | `cam6` | `cam7`, `cam8` | 120 | 47,374 | 5.87%, 7.57% | −5.87% | 5.9 | 3.12%, 5.45% | +2.75% | 11.3 |
| private scene A | the five cameras | – | 600 | 61,506 | 4.02%, 6.07% | – | 4.1 | 4.57%, 8.82% | – | 19.2 |

- **The floor.** On `kitchen` a pixel's two depths differ by 0.09% at the median on static pixels and 0.15% on the dynamic objects, 3 to 5 mm at 3.6 m, for cameras 3° to 7° apart, which triangulate less sharply than private scene A's 15° would.
- **On private scene A they differ by 4%.** On the person, 4.0% of the depth at the median, 4.1 cm, and by camera 1.4% to 6.5%, 1.5 to 7.9 cm: 27 times the floor of `kitchen`'s dynamic objects. On the background, 4.6%.
- **It is an offset, not a scatter.** For `cam3`, `cam4` and `cam6` the signed figure equals the unsigned one: at the median frame one partner places the whole person nearer than the other does, by 3.9% to 6.5%. The 90th percentile is not far above the median (6.1% against 4.0%).
- **Most of it is one fixed relation per camera.** A baseline or a focal length that is not the manifest's scales every depth a pair triangulates, and a turn along the epipolar lines adds one amount to every inverse depth. So errors of the cameras relate one pair's inverse depths to the other's by a scale a and a shift b that do not change with the frame: 1/z₂ = a/z₁ + b (row 625). Fitted once per camera over all its frames, with the alignment's robust fit and with the person and the background weighing alike in every frame:

| Scene | Camera | Its two partners | Before: person or dynamic objects; background or static pixels | Scale a | Shift b (per unit of depth) | After: person or dynamic objects, median; a frame's median, least to most | After: background or static pixels, median; a frame's median, least to most |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `kitchen` | the nine combinations | – | 0.13% to 0.19%; 0.07% to 0.12% | 0.9996 to 1.0001 | −0.0001 to +0.0003 | 0.13% to 0.17%; 0.09% to 0.26% | 0.07% to 0.10%; 0.07% to 0.11% |
| private scene A | `cam1` | `cam0`, `cam4` | 1.34%; 1.73% | 1.009 | −0.0021 | 1.60%; 0.57% to 5.26% | 1.67%; 1.11% to 6.08% |
| private scene A | `cam3` | `cam4`, `cam5` | 6.46%; 5.39% | 1.077 | −0.0045 | 1.30%; 0.66% to 2.77% | 6.84%; 3.66% to 21.94% |
| private scene A | `cam4` | `cam1`, `cam3` | 3.88%; 5.24% | 0.971 | −0.0073 | 1.01%; 0.55% to 2.35% | 2.55%; 1.89% to 6.40% |
| private scene A | `cam5` | `cam2`, `cam3` | 1.55%; 4.99% | 1.010 | −0.0098 | 1.64%; 1.02% to 3.16% | 2.00%; 1.47% to 2.83% |
| private scene A | `cam6` | `cam7`, `cam8` | 5.84%; 3.07% | 1.099 | −0.0363 | 1.51%; 0.66% to 3.74% | 3.67%; 0.11% to 6.46% |

- **What the fit does.** On `kitchen` it finds nothing to remove (a within 0.0004 of 1). On private scene A the scale runs from 0.97 to 1.10 and the shift from −0.002 to −0.036 per unit of depth, which at a baseline of 0.27 is a turn of 0.03° to 0.56°. After it the person's two depths differ by 1.0% to 1.6% at the median frame, by camera, where they differed by 1.3% to 6.5%; `cam1` and `cam5`, which had little offset, gain nothing.
- **What that relation is, the probe does not say.** It is what wrong cameras would give, and four of its five shifts are no larger than the turns section 2.5 fitted. But a scale of 8% to 10% between two pairs (`cam3`, `cam6`) would be an error of more than 2 cm in a baseline of 27 cm, and a person drawn a little nearer for one pair than for the other, alike through the clip, would look the same. For `cam3` and `cam6` the relation that suits the person leaves the background at 6.8% and 3.7%, which an error of the cameras would not; yet with D-22's 2 px filter on, `cam6`'s background follows the person's relation to 2.3%. So neither reading is excluded.
- **What remains changes with the frame.** After the fit the person's two depths still differ by 1.0% to 1.6% of the depth, 1.1 to 1.7 cm, 7 to 11 times `kitchen`'s floor, and a frame's median runs from 0.55% to 5.3%. An error of the cameras is the same in every frame, and the part of a turn that a scale and a shift do not capture, which grows toward the image's edges, comes to a few tenths of a percent at most with these shifts. So most of this part is the images': the person drawn at depths that two pairs do not share, by 2 to 3 px along the epipolar line (5 mm of depth per pixel, "How scale and shift are set"). It is the size of section 2.5's residual across the line (0.9 to 5.2 px by pair), in the direction that residual does not read.
- **A prior map inherits it whole.** One camera's map at one frame, aligned once on each of its two pairs' anchors, is lifted to two depths that differ by 4.2% at the person's median depth with the regional alignment and 4.0% with the whole-image one (5.8% for `cam3` and `cam6`, 0.9% for `cam5`), against 0.11% and 0.06% on `kitchen` (`two_pairs.py`, from the scales and shifts of the runs above). The map is the same in both, so all of that difference comes from the anchors.
- **For initialization,** this says that the init points of two pairs that share a camera lie on two surfaces of the person some centimetres apart before training starts. With D-22's own filters, which init points pass, the person's two depths differ by 4.2% at the median, 1.3% to 6.9% and 1.5 to 8.0 cm by camera, over the 484 of the 600 images that keep points on the person. The shell sits 5 to 25 cm from the body (section 2.1).

**Private scene A: the variants.** How much the person's figures hang on three choices, over the 7 pairs together and every frame. The first two rows are the tables above.

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

| regional | D-22's own | the manifest's | rule a | 2.2, 11.2 | 2.04%, 9.22% | 1.94%, 8.90% | 1.94%, 8.90% | 1.83%, 8.81% | 2.35%, 9.73% | 2.44%, 8.49% | 795 of 840 |

| regional | without the 2 px filter | turned by section 2.5's fit | rule a | 2.1, 12.8 | 2.00%, 10.53% | 1.82%, 9.22% | 1.82%, 9.22% | 1.84%, 7.92% | 2.22%, 13.79% | 2.44%, 8.55% | 840 of 840 |
| whole image | without the 2 px filter | the manifest's | rule b | 2.3, 18.1 | 1.94%, 12.37% | 1.90%, 12.96% | 1.70%, 6.86% | 1.80%, 6.78% | 2.21%, 38.98% | 8.30%, 82.60% | 840 of 840 |
| regional | without the 2 px filter | the manifest's | rule b | 2.3, 16.7 | 2.17%, 12.92% | 1.96%, 11.06% | 1.96%, 11.06% | 1.94%, 9.07% | 2.49%, 18.16% | 2.49%, 8.31% | 840 of 840 |

- **D-22's own anchors** leave the median over the 7 pairs where it is (1.85% with the whole-image alignment, 2.04% with the regional one). A pair's median moves by up to 0.6% of the depth either way, and `cam1`–`cam4`'s by 1.2%: up with the whole-image alignment, to 3.4%, and down with the regional one, to 3.5%, which then has no figure for the person in 45 of that pair's frames.
- **Camera B turned.** Section 2.5 fitted, per pair, a turn of camera B of 0.10° to 0.41° that brings the background's epipolar residual down. Here camera B is turned by it before anything else, so that the anchors are triangulated again with the turned camera. D-22's filters would then keep more of the matches in `cam1`–`cam4` (67% from 47%) and `cam3`–`cam4` (73% from 58%), and fewer in `cam6`–`cam7` (47% from 58%) and `cam6`–`cam8` (54% from 68%), as section 2.5 found the person's residual to do. The person's distance moves by less than 0.1% of the depth (1.79% from 1.85%, 2.00% from 2.02%), and no pair's median by more than 0.2%. So the tenths of a degree by which section 2.5 found the manifest's cameras off are not what the person's pairwise figures are made of, as the check on `kitchen` predicted. A turn along the epipolar lines, which that fit holds at zero, would not show here either.
- **Rule b's crop** takes in more of each image (the person is 31.2% of an image's pixels at the median, against 28.8%) and raises the median by 0.1% of the depth (1.94% and 2.17%; no pair's by more than 0.4%) and the 90th percentile by 2 to 3% (12.4% and 12.9%); in front with both ends on the person the 90th percentile is 6.9% with the whole-image alignment, against 6.1%. The rectangle matters to the tail and little to the median.

**What the figures mean for H2's premise.** H2 holds that a depth estimated on each generated camera would inherit the cameras' disagreement about the person. The figures split that in two. Where a lifted surface lies, it takes from its alignment, and there it inherits the disagreement whole. What shape it has, it takes from the estimator, and there the probe finds the estimator's own error and cannot see the cameras' disagreement under it.

- **The floor.** On `kitchen`, whose cameras agree exactly, Depth Anything V2 Small's lifted depth is off the ground truth by about 1% of the depth on static pixels and by 1.5% on the dynamic objects once they have a scale and a shift of their own (3.7% without). Two cameras 15° apart then place the same surface 0.84% of the depth apart on static pixels, and the dynamic objects 2.7% apart with the whole-image alignment (2.4% with both ends on them) and 1.33% with the regional one.
- **The person's shape, against that floor.** Aligned pair by pair, the two cameras' surfaces of the person lie 1.85% of the depth apart at the median with the whole-image alignment (1.73% with both ends on the person) and 2.02% with the regional one: 0.7 times `kitchen`'s dynamic objects under the first and 1.5 times under the second. Neither alignment is the like-for-like one beyond doubt, since the person holds most of an image's anchors and `kitchen`'s dynamic objects a few percent, so the two bracket the answer. The five side-by-side pairs read 0.93% to 2.10% with the regional alignment. The two pairs whose cameras sit one above the other read 4.6% and 4.8%, and `kitchen`, whose cameras lie on one arc, has no pair like them to set beside.
- **The cap on what the shape inherits.** Between nothing and 0.7% of the depth at the median, under 1 cm. In the part of the tail that neither occlusion nor a point leaving the person explains, in front with both ends on the person, between nothing and a factor of 1.8 at the 90th percentile (6.1% against `kitchen`'s 7.25% with the whole-image alignment, 9.3% against 5.04% with the regional one, where the two stacked pairs make the difference). And the scene's own controls say that an excess of this size need not come from the cameras: on the background the maps agree three times less well between cameras than on `kitchen`, and four times less well between two frames of one camera.
- **The person's position.** Two pairs that share a camera triangulate the same pixel of the person to depths 4.0% apart at the median, 4.1 cm, 27 times `kitchen`'s floor, and a prior map aligned on either pair's anchors is lifted to that pair's depth: the same map lands 4.2% apart under its two alignments. So whatever a prior map is aligned to on this scene, the init points or a depth rendered from primitives that grew from them, it inherits a disagreement in depth of some 4 cm between pairs. The estimator adds nothing to that and removes nothing from it: its output has no scale and no shift of its own. Most of the 4% follows one fixed relation per camera, such as wrong cameras would give; 1.0% to 1.6% does not, and changes with the frame.
- **Better, the same or worse than the images.** Measured in depth, and each after its own alignment: the images, three at a time, disagree about the person by 1.0% to 1.6% once one fixed relation per camera is taken out (4.0% before), 7 to 11 times their floor (27 times before); the prior maps of two cameras disagree by 1.7% to 2.0%, 0.7 to 1.5 times theirs. In size, then, about the same, the maps a little worse. Against their floors the maps are far better, which only says that this estimator's own error between two cameras is as large as the disagreement it would have to show; and the maps say nothing about where the person is. Section 2.5's own figure cannot be set beside these directly: its 2 to 10 mm is a distance across the epipolar line, sideways in the image, and a surface that faces the camera can be drawn shifted sideways without any change of depth.
- **The part section 2.5 could not see.** Of the person's kept pixels, 73% have no admitted match. There the two surfaces lie 2.0% to 2.4% of the depth apart at the median, 2.4 to 2.6 cm: the bulk of the unmatched part is not a shell's distance apart in the prior maps. A tenth of it is more than 19 to 22 cm apart, and `kitchen`'s unmatched pixels have such a tail without any disagreement, from occlusion. So the probe finds no sign of a disagreement of 5 to 25 cm in shape hidden where the matcher gives up, and cannot exclude one on a tenth of those pixels.
- **What a technique would meet.** Three things, for section 3.4 to weigh. A depth prior from this estimator, aligned per image or per region, leaves the two cameras of a pair with surfaces of the person 2 cm apart at the median and 13 cm at the 90th percentile, of which most or all is the estimator's own error, as on `kitchen`. Aligned to the init points it is left with a misfit of 1.8% of the depth on the person, and with one scale and shift for the whole image, 4.8% on the person and 13.5% on the background, with opposite signs: a loss that fits one scale and shift per image does not hold person and background together on this scene. And no alignment of a relative depth can settle the 4 cm by which the pairs disagree in depth: that part of the disagreement reaches training through the images and the init points whether a prior map is used or not.

So the premise holds for position and is not seen for shape. A depth prior per camera would inherit the cameras' disagreement in depth exactly as far as it is aligned to anything triangulated or rendered, and beyond that it brings an error of its own of about 2% of the depth between cameras, which is the size of the estimator's error on `kitchen` too. For the recommendation this points the same way as H2: a surface constraint built on per-camera depth does not remove the disagreement, and needs beside it a way to absorb or down-weight what the cameras do not share. Most of the 4% follows one fixed relation per camera, which a correction of each camera could remove if it is the cameras' (section 3.6); whether one set of corrected cameras brings all the pairs together was not tested here, and is the first thing to settle. About 1% to 1.6% of the depth would remain either way.

**What the probe cannot show.**

- **Position, within a pair.** The two images of a pair are aligned on the same triangulated points, so a common error of those points moves both surfaces alike. A camera turned by 0.3° along the epipolar lines moves both by 3% of the depth and leaves 0.3% between them. The pairwise figures say how far the two surfaces are from each other, not how far either is from the person; "One camera, two partners" is the only reading of position here, and it compares pairs with each other, not with the truth: a relation that all pairs share does not show in it.
- **Sideways disagreement.** The measure is a gap in depth. A part of the person drawn shifted across the image in one camera, which is what section 2.5's residual reads, changes the lifted depth only where the surface slopes or ends, so it hardly enters the measure away from the person's outline.
- **The estimator's share.** One estimator was run. Its paper says its synthetic training sets hold no people (row 521), and nothing here separates an error it makes on a generated person from a disagreement of the images in shape. A second estimator that passes the bar would: DAViD's depth model, made for people, or Video Depth Anything Small, which shares one scale and shift over a camera's video (section 3.3). Neither was run, and DAViD has no floor on `kitchen`, whose subject is not a person.
- **Cameras one above the other.** `kitchen`'s 20 cameras lie on one arc, so the two stacked pairs of private scene A, which read highest, have no floor of their own.
- **The person's outline.** The person is the pixels of a rectangle lifted to a near depth, and the rectangle is the tools' default on seven cameras. No image and no prior map was looked at, so whether those pixels are the person, and all of the person, rests on the numbers above and on the owner's check of section 2.5's sheet, which is pending.
- **Other sizes of the estimator.** The Base and Large models fail the bar (section 3.3) and were not run; the floor is the Small model's.

**Runs.** `python` is the shared `.venv`'s, with `OMP_NUM_THREADS`, `MKL_NUM_THREADS` and `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS` at the thread count given. `$T` is `<scratch>/ticket-12`, `$T4` is `<scratch>/ticket-4`, `$O` is `<surface-runs>/ticket-12`, `$O4` is `<surface-runs>/ticket-4/private-scene-A`, and `$G` is `$OPENFTGS_DATASETS/synth/ground_truth/kitchen`. The estimator runs with `python -I` and `HF_HUB_OFFLINE=1 TRANSFORMERS_OFFLINE=1 HF_HUB_DISABLE_TELEMETRY=1`. The matcher's runs have `PYTHONPATH=<worktree>/src:<worktree>/harness/src:<worktree>/scripts:$T4/env-init` and `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1`, so that nothing is written into ticket #4's folder; `agree.py`'s have `PYTHONPATH=<worktree>/src:<worktree>/harness/src:<worktree>/scripts`. The GPU ran one process at a time.

| Command | Threads; device | What it ran; time | Note |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `curl -L -A "research-client/1.0" -o $T/weights/depth_anything_v2_vits.pth https://huggingface.co/depth-anything/Depth-Anything-V2-Small/resolve/main/depth_anything_v2_vits.pth` | 1; CPU | 99,218,434 bytes; 3 s | the weights file, without credentials; its SHA-256 is in "The estimator" (row 618) |

| the same with `--tag wide --pairs cam02:cam06,cam03:cam07,cam05:cam09,cam06:cam10,cam08:cam12,cam12:cam16`; and with `--tag wide-next --frames 1:300:100` and the same pairs | 4; GPU | 180 evaluations, 1 min 32 s; 18 evaluations, 15 s | the six pairs about 15° apart; then their frames 1, 101 and 201. A first try that also named `cam00:cam01` stopped at once: that script takes its cameras from the init frames, which hold no test camera (D-66) |
| `python $T/match_pair.py <m15-runs>/kitchen/scene.json $O/kitchen --label kitchen --tag cam00 --pair cam00:cam01 --frames 0:300:10 --device cuda:0` | 4; GPU | 30 evaluations; 24 s | the test camera's pair, by the same matcher builder and settings, in the same grid format |

| `python -I $T/two_pairs.py $O/results` | 1; CPU | under 1 s | one prior map under its two pairs' alignments, from the scales and shifts the runs above saved |
| `$T/make_tables.sh $O/results $T/tables`, which runs `python -I $T/doc_tables.py $O/results --only <table>` per table; `python -I $T/tables.py $O/results $O/checks --only checks` | 1; CPU | seconds | the tables of this section |

The GPU was in use for about 67 minutes in all: 1 minute by the estimator, 6 by the matcher and 60 by `agree.py`. Of those 60, 36 are the runs above and 24 are earlier passes that were set aside and are not reported: trial runs on a few frames, and a first pass of the regional alignment that took a region's pixels, and then the guard, from the regional alignment's own depth, which a scale and a shift fitted to the person distort everywhere else. The runs above take both from the whole-image alignment. `agree.py` took 0.2 to 0.45 s per pair-frame. No output file holds private scene A's location or name: the scene is named by `--label`.

## 3. The screening tables

Six sections, one per ticket of the survey, with the same tables and the same columns throughout, so that section 4 can put them together. This introduction fixes the method, the columns and how a cell is written; it is the PRD's "The search map", "Method", "Screening columns" and "Clean room for the search", with what tickets #6 to #11 add to them, and the PRD and the tickets win where this differs.

**The families and their search seeds.** Names not marked as the owner's seeds are search terms from the implementer's memory (row 502): none is a finding until a section reads it. Third-party method names are spelled as their authors spell them.

| Family | Section | What to find | Search seeds |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A. Surface-following primitives | 3.1 | Flat or planar primitives; the shortest axis as the normal; flattening and scale losses; consistency between depth and normals; depth distortion; geometric consistency between cameras; oriented initialization | 2D Gaussian Splatting, Gaussian Surfels, SuGaR, PGSR, RaDe-GS, Gaussian Opacity Fields |
| B. Opacity and floater regularizers | 3.2 | Opacity entropy or binarization; opacity decay; anisotropy and rank constraints; other regularizers aimed at faint primitives off the surface | 4C4D (the owner's seed, arXiv 2604.04063, row 493); 3DGS-MCMC's regularizers |
| C. Prior maps from image networks | 3.3 (the estimators), 3.4 (the techniques) | Monocular and video depth; normal estimators, including those made for people; feed-forward point maps; 4D geometry with scene flow; the losses that use them (scale-invariant, ranking, correlation); position and velocity initialized from them | MotionCrafter (the owner's seed, arXiv 2602.08961, row 491); DN-Splatter, DNGaussian, the DUSt3R and VGGT line |
| D. Dynamic-scene surfaces | 3.5 | Dynamic surfels and meshes; local rigidity; shared motion; orientation that changes with time; training from few cameras; FreeTimeGS follow-ups | Split4D (DOI 10.1145/3763343, row 494) and the survey arXiv 2609.39960 with its list (rows 492, 495), the owner's seeds; Dynamic 3D Gaussians, SC-GS, 4D-Rotor GS, Vidu4D, MonoFusion |
| E. Cameras whose images disagree (adjacent) | 3.6 | Reconstruction from generated images; robust and uncertainty-weighted losses; per-camera correction; weighting cameras or pixels by trust; leaving pixels known to be wrong out of the loss; feature-space losses | CAT4D, SpotLessSplats, WildGaussians |

The list's table of Gaussian-splatting methods has columns for flow, normals, uncertainty and extra priors, but only two of its 60 rows tick normals (row 495), so families A to C are expected to come mostly from static-scene work and from citations.

**Method.**

- **Routes:** arXiv abstract pages and listings; web search; proceedings and project pages; Hugging Face paper pages; backward references through Crossref and the papers' own lists; forward citations through Semantic Scholar or SciX. Google Scholar has no API: it is reached through web search, or by the owner checking a paper's citing list by hand. On 2026-11-13 the keyless arXiv API, Semantic Scholar and OpenAlex returned rate-limit errors, and arXiv abstract pages and Crossref answered (rows 491 to 494, 500).
- **Funnel:** title, then abstract, then a full read of the method, the experiments that isolate it and the limitations, then a row of the screening table.
- **Stop rule, per family:** two consecutive search rounds that add no technique passing the abstract screen, or the cap of 60 abstracts and 12 full reads; 120 and 24 for family E; 360 and 72 overall (decision 1). The owner can move the caps. A search that failed or was rate-limited is not a round for the stop rule.
- **Recency round:** one round per family restricted to 2025-10 onward, including the papers that cite FreeTimeGS.
- **The estimators** (section 3.3) are screened by kind, not by this funnel: at most six estimators per kind, those the Gaussian-splatting literature of 2024 to 2026 uses most and the newest that pass the bar; a kind stops after two consecutive search rounds that add no estimator, or at six. Reading a paper's abstract to place an estimator is logged but doesn't count toward a family's cap.
- **Hand-overs.** A technique that belongs to another family is named where it was met, with that family, and its row is left to that family's section. Losses that need a prior map go to section 3.4; flattening, and consistency between depth and normals, that need no prior map go to section 3.1; opacity regularizers go to section 3.2. 4C4D's row is section 3.2's, and section 3.5 names it for its training from few cameras. MotionCrafter the estimator is screened in section 3.3, and its point maps and scene flow as an initialization of position and velocity in section 3.4.
- **Besides its tables,** a family's section says for each search seed where its row is or why it has none, records the recency round, lists the techniques handed to other families, and says how the family reached its stop rule.

**Clean room for the search.** Searches may name FreeTimeGS, and papers that cite it are read. Code that implements or extends it is never opened, Split4D's included; a hit that looks like such code gets a "not opened" source-log row. EasyVolcap is never opened. Each project's license file is read before anything else of it; source code is read only under a permissive license that allows commercial use, and a repository under a copyleft, non-commercial or missing license is documentation only, its method taken from its paper. gsplat is read at the pinned 1.5.3 only; `gsplat/contrib/dynamic` and dynamic-scene code on newer branches stay unopened. Weights' terms come only from the weights' publisher: its README, model card, license file or release page. No repository-indexing tools. Third-party text is paraphrased, never pasted. Keys live in environment variables and are never committed. No request carries the owner's name or email, this project's name or anything about private scene A.

**The screening columns**, tightest first. Every screening table has these ten columns, the technique and the PRD's nine, under these headers:

| Header | What the cell holds |
| --- | --- |
| Technique | The technique's name, its paper and year |
| 1. Weights' license | For each estimator the technique uses: its weights' license per weights file, from its publisher, with the training data recorded, and its verdict against the bar. The verdict is cited from the estimators table that holds it (section 3.3, or the family's own) |
| 2. Code license | The code's license, and whether the code is readable or the technique is taken from its paper only |
| 3. Integration class | One of five classes, in these words: a loss on today's primitives; a loss that needs new render outputs; a change to growth, relocation or split; a change to a quantity or to motion; new rasterization kernels. With it, whether the field's default leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched |
| 4. Render backends | What it needs from the render backends (which depth, normals, distortion), and whether gsplat 1.5.3's stage functions and the reference backend can supply it, cited from section 2.4 |
| 5. Fit with the primitives | Its fit with constant orientation and scale, E1's linear motion, short durations, relocation (E10, E11) and split (E17) |
| 6. Cameras that disagree | Whether it assumes cameras whose images agree and are right in every pixel, and any evidence with few cameras, inconsistent images or pixels known to be wrong |
| 7. Prior maps | The prior maps to precompute (depth, normals, masks, flow, point maps), their cost, and whether maps the user supplies would do |
| 8. Evidence | The data, the metric, and an experiment that isolates the technique |
| 9. Training cost | Its training cost against the default training configuration, whose per-iteration cost by stage is B-1's (§3.4) |

**The estimators tables.** Section 3.3 holds family C's estimators table. An estimator a technique names that section 3.3 lacks is screened in its family's own table, "Estimators first met in this family", with the same columns and the same bar. One row per weights file:

| Header | What the cell holds |
| --- | --- |
| Kind | The kind of prior map: monocular depth; video depth; surface normals; depth and normals made for people; feed-forward point maps; 4D geometry with scene flow; optical flow and point tracks; masks (segmentation and matting) |
| Estimator | The estimator's name, its paper and year |
| Weights file | The file, or the release, the row's terms apply to |
| Code license | The license of the estimator's code |
| Weights: the publisher's statement, with its URL | What the weights' own publisher states about that file's license, and where |
| Training data | The data the weights were trained on, recorded and not judged |
| Bar | Pass or fail against the bar, with the reason |
| Size; cost per frame | The parameters and the cost per frame, as the publisher states them |
| Supplied maps | Whether prior maps the user supplies as files would do in the estimator's place |

**The search-round logs.** One row per search round. In a family's log: the round's number, its date, its route, its query, the number of results seen, how many passed the title screen (their abstracts were read, and count toward the family's cap), how many passed the abstract screen (techniques new to the survey; a round that has none counts toward the stop rule), how many were read in full (they count toward the cap of full reads), how many rows of the screening table came of it, and a note: the recency round, a failed or rate-limited search, a hand-over. Section 3.3's log is by kind: the kind, the round, its date, its route, its query, the results seen, the estimators added and a note.

**How a cell is written.**

- Every cell is filled. Where a column doesn't apply the cell says "none" and why, as "none: no estimator"; where the source read doesn't say, "not stated".
- Every fact in a cell carries its source-log row, as `(row 499)` or `(rows 492, 495)`. On a ticket's branch a new row is cited by its temporary ID in the same form, `T`, the ticket's number, a point and the row's counter, and written in no other way, so that the merge can replace every one with its number.
- A verdict or an answer taken from this document is cited by its section: the bar's verdict from section 3.3 or the family's own estimators table, the render backends' answer from section 2.4.
- A cell that is the ticket's own reading of the spec or of this repository's code (columns 3 and 5, and parts of 4 and 9) cites the spec item or the Q-n row it rests on, which need no source-log row, beside the row of the paper the technique's needs were read from.
- Nothing is filled from memory. A claim with no source read is left out, or logged as "No source read: general knowledge" and marked so in the cell.
- Third-party text is paraphrased, never pasted.

### 3.1 Family A: surface-following primitives

*Ticket #7's section.* The survey of family A, with the cap of 60 abstracts and 12 full reads.

Thirty-two abstracts were read and twelve papers read in full, on 2026-11-15 and 16; the search-round log and the paragraphs under it account for each. The screening table has one row per technique, not per paper, because the search seeds bundle parts whose integration class differs: a paper's flat primitive is a change to a quantity, the loss it trains with needs new render outputs, and a term that reads only the scales is a loss on today's primitives. "The search seeds", below, says which rows hold each seed. Three things hold for every row:

- **Today's primitive**, which columns 3 to 5 are judged against, is a 3D Gaussian whose orientation and scale are constant in time (§1.1) and are set once, by `FreeTimeGaussians.from_init_points` in `openftgs.model`: one scale for the three axes and the identity quaternion (§1.3 steps 8 and 9, D-5, D-25). `roma_init` returns init points with no normal (section 2.3). So at the start no axis of a primitive is shorter than another, and "the shortest axis" is a three-way tie.
- **The vector taken as a primitive's normal** differs between techniques, and column 5 names it for each: the rotation's third column where one scale is dropped or pinned (2DGS, Gaussian Surfels, GeoGaussian); the direction of the smallest scale where the three scales stay free (PGSR, SuGaR, TrimGS, and CoMVS-GS, which starts that direction on the third column); a vector that depends on the ray or the camera and is no axis of the primitive (Gaussian Opacity Fields, RaDe-GS). The normal of a depth map is another thing again, taken from finite differences of the rendered depth.
- **A cell marked "this ticket's reading"** is this ticket's own reading of the spec, of section 2.4 or of a paper's equations, and was not run. Nothing here was trained or rendered.

**Screening table.**

| Technique | 1. Weights' license | 2. Code license | 3. Integration class | 4. Render backends | 5. Fit with the primitives | 6. Cameras that disagree | 7. Prior maps | 8. Evidence | 9. Training cost |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Flattening loss on the smallest scale** (PGSR, Chen et al., 2024, arXiv 2406.06521, which takes it from NeuSG, arXiv 2312.00846, not read) | none: no estimator (row 631) | Paper only: PGSR's repository is under its authors' own license, for educational, research and non-profit use, with commercial use by arrangement (row 636). The term is one equation of the paper (row 631) | **A loss on today's primitives:** the L1 norm of the smallest of a primitive's three scales, weighted 100 beside the rendering loss (Eq. 1; row 631). It reads `log_scales` and nothing a render returns. A weight that defaults to 0 leaves E9's value, E1 to E17 and the §3 tests as they are; the field is a new `LossConfig` field, a §2.1 signature (section 2.2) | Nothing: no depth, normal or distortion. It never reaches a render backend, so both behave alike (row 631) | The normal it implies is the direction of the smallest scale, turned against the viewing direction (row 631). The scales it shrinks are the stored, constant ones (§1.1): it fits. At the start the smallest scale is a three-way tie at the identity rotation (D-5, D-25), so which axis flattens is arbitrary and its turn to the surface is left to the rendering loss; CoMVS-GS says the same of planar methods that start from isotropic spheres (row 646), and the row on oriented initialization is the remedy the papers give. E11 rescales the three scales by one factor and E17's children take a/1.6 on all three, so both keep a primitive's flatness; both draw the new positions from the parent's Gaussian, so the copies of a flat parent lie within its thickness of its plane; the clone-or-split test reads the largest scale, which the loss leaves alone (this ticket's reading of E11, E17 and §1.3 Growth). Under E1 a flat primitive keeps its orientation while it moves: it follows a surface that translates, not one that turns. Over durations of one to four frames (section 2.6) that costs little, and it costs more the longer a primitive lives. Whether the term runs over all N primitives or weights them by σt(t), as E8 does, the paper cannot say: a static method has no such choice | It compares neither cameras nor pixels, so it assumes nothing of the images. For the same reason it cannot tell a flat primitive on the surface from a flat one off it: alone it shapes the shell's primitives and does not move them (this ticket's reading). No evidence with few cameras or inconsistent images in the papers read. SparseSurf's abstract says the stronger anisotropy of flattened Gaussians worsens overfitting when views are sparse (row 642) | none: no prior map (row 631) | **Not isolated.** PGSR's ablation on Tanks and Temples removes the single-view loss, the multi-view losses, the occlusion weight, the plane-distance depth and the exposure model, and has no row without the flattening loss (row 631). CoMVS-GS keeps it at the same weight from the first iteration (row 646). SharpTimeGS, a 4D Gaussian method evaluated on Neural3DV, ENeRF-Outdoor and SelfCap, adds a scale term "similar to PGSR" to its regularization, and its ablation does not isolate it either (row 647). The nearest figure is indirect: 2DGS's additional baselines render disks through 3DGS's own affine rasterization, with one scale of the 3D Gaussian fixed at 10⁻⁶, and reach a Chamfer distance of 1.08 on DTU, against 1.96 for 3DGS and 0.88 for 2DGS's rasterizer, all meshed from mean depth (Table 6; row 628). Section 3.4's read of VCR-GauS, which adds such a scale loss, records the one ablation of it: F-score 0.36 without the scale loss and 0.40 with it on Tanks and Temples (row 726; row C8 of section 4.8) | A minimum over three scales per primitive: negligible beside E8's mean over all N (this ticket's reading; no paper times it alone); PGSR gives no time for the term (row 631) |
| **Co-planarity loss between neighbouring primitives** (GeoGaussian, Li et al., ECCV 2024, arXiv 2403.11324) | none: no estimator (row 645) | Paper only: the repository is under the non-commercial Gaussian-Splatting License, with one MIT-derived file noted (row 651) | **A loss on today's primitives:** for each thin primitive visible in the training image and its eight nearest neighbours whose normals agree within an angle, the absolute difference between its plane offset r₃ᵀμ and the neighbours' mean offset, weighted 0.05, plus the distance between its normal and theirs, weighted 0.01 (Eq. 6; row 645). It reads `means`, `quats` and a neighbour list, and no render output. A weight that defaults to 0 leaves E9, E1 to E17 and the §3 tests; the field is a new `LossConfig` field, and the neighbour list a new piece of training state | Nothing from the render. It applies to the primitives visible in the current image, which `RenderOutput`'s `visible` already gives (section 2.3) | The normal is the rotation's third column, the axis GeoGaussian pins thin (row 645). Today's scales are free, so that axis would have to be the smallest-scale direction, or one pinned by the row on oriented initialization. The paper refreshes the neighbour list every 100 iterations, then every 1,000. In a 4D model the neighbours of a primitive are those alive at the same time: a search per time, or in space and time, which the paper, a static method, does not have (this ticket's reading). Under E1 the plane offset is taken at μ(t). E11's and E17's copies start as near neighbours of their parent with its orientation, so they satisfy the term at birth | It assumes nothing between cameras: it is a smoothness prior between primitives. It would pull a shell's primitives toward a common plane with their neighbours, whether or not that plane is the body's surface (this ticket's reading). The paper's limits: the constraint degenerates where few smooth regions are found, and a false smooth region gives wrong residuals; its scenes are rooms with walls and furniture (row 645) | none: no prior map (row 645) | **Not isolated.** The lines read hold no ablation that removes the loss, the initialization or the densification alone. For the whole method, novel views of Replica's R1 sequence trained on 10 % of its images score 31.65 dB against 30.49 for 3DGS, and 38.00 against 37.45 on 50 %; a training curve shows 3DGS overfitting the training views where GeoGaussian does not (Table 4, Figure 5; row 645) | A neighbour search over the thin primitives every 100 iterations and a term on the visible ones: not timed in the lines read (row 645) |
| **Depth–normal consistency loss** (2DGS, Huang et al., 2024, arXiv 2403.17888; other forms in Gaussian Surfels, PGSR, Gaussian Opacity Fields, RaDe-GS and TrimGS) | none: no estimator (rows 628, 629, 631, 632, 633, 649) | Paper only. The repositories of 2DGS, Gaussian Opacity Fields and RaDe-GS are under the non-commercial Gaussian-Splatting License and PGSR's under its authors' non-commercial one; those of Gaussian Surfels and TrimGS carry no license (rows 634, 637, 638, 636, 639, 652). gsplat 1.5.3 returns both normal maps for flat primitives under Apache-2.0 (section 2.4) | **A loss that needs new render outputs:** per pixel, the blending-weighted sum of 1 − nᵢᵀN, nᵢ a normal per primitive and N the normal of the rendered depth map (2DGS's Eq. 14; row 628). Gaussian Surfels and PGSR compare the two maps after blending, PGSR weighted down where the training image has an edge, and TrimGS takes an L1 between them (rows 629, 631, 649). Default off leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests. The outputs are new `RenderOutput` fields, a §2.1 change with new lines in U-7 and U-12, or Q-226's device with no API change (section 2.4) | Two outputs. **A composited normal** from a vector per primitive: section 2.4, cell 4a (channels of the colour pass), cell 4d (the device, a pass of its own) and cell 4e (the reference backend). **A normal from the depth map:** cells 5a, 5d and 5e, on expected depth (cells 2a, 2d and 2e). So gsplat 1.5.3's stage functions and the reference backend can supply an expected-depth form for today's primitives. The papers differ in the depth they take. 2DGS takes N at the median intersection and TrimGS from median depth (rows 628, 649): cells 3a and 3d say no for E6's render, cell 3c yes for flat primitives, and the pair-list route is unmeasured (the owner's open question). Gaussian Surfels divide blended depth by alpha, and PGSR uses its plane-distance depth (rows 629, 631), so the loss is published in forms that need no median. And E6 holds one depth per primitive, its centre's, constant across the footprint, which Gaussian Surfels and RaDe-GS show to disagree with a slanted primitive's normal (rows 629, 632); their per-pixel depths come from changed kernels, PGSR's from composited constants (the row on plane-distance depth) | The vector taken as nᵢ: the rotation's third column turned to the camera for 2DGS's disks, and the same column for Gaussian Surfels; the direction of the smallest scale for PGSR and TrimGS, on 3D Gaussians; the normal of a plane that depends on the ray or the camera for Gaussian Opacity Fields and RaDe-GS (rows 628, 629, 631, 649, 633, 632). On today's primitives the smallest-scale direction is the one that needs no kernel. Gaussian Opacity Fields measured it against its own definition: an F-score of 0.40 against 0.46 on Tanks and Temples (row 633). It is undefined while a primitive is a sphere (D-5, D-25), so the loss wants primitives already flattened or a late start: RaDe-GS and CoMVS-GS turn it on at iteration 15,000 and 7,000 of 30,000, and Gaussian Surfels raise its weight from 0 (rows 632, 646, 629). Its gradient reaches `quats` through nᵢ and `means` through the depth, at the training image's time only. Constant orientation and E1: as the row on the flattening loss. Through the device the term does not enter E10's g unless a prototype adds it (section 2.4) | A single-view term: it compares a render with itself and assumes nothing between cameras. It assumes one opaque surface per pixel (2DGS's stated limit; row 628). Few cameras: Gaussian Surfels, as a whole method, trained on half of DTU's images keep 31.70 dB where 3DGS falls to 30.08 (row 629), and 4DSurf keeps the term on 8 and 10 cameras around people (row 648). Inconsistent images: no evidence | none: no prior map. PGSR's edge weight reads the training image itself (row 631) | **Isolated in five papers, on static scenes.** 2DGS, DTU, 15,000 iterations: Chamfer distance 1.24 without it against 0.83 (Table 5; row 628). Gaussian Surfels, DTU: 1.243 against 0.882, and 31.56 dB against 32.51 (Table 4; row 629). Gaussian Opacity Fields, Tanks and Temples: F-score 0.37 against 0.46 (Table 4; row 633). PGSR's single-view loss, Tanks and Temples: 0.49 against 0.52 (Table IV; row 631). TrimGS, DTU, on 3D Gaussians with the smallest-scale normal and median depth, fine-tuned 7,000 iterations: the mesh's Chamfer distance goes from 1.19 to 0.83 when the term is added, while the figure of the paper's point-based evaluation goes from 1.27 to 1.32 (Table 5; row 649). In the dynamic methods read it is a standing term that no ablation removes (rows 647, 648). 2DGS, PGSR and TrimGS each report that geometry terms cost a little image quality (rows 628, 631, 649) | As channels of the colour pass, depth and a normal cost 3.2 ms on kitchen, 10 to 13 % of an iteration, the normal's own computation not included; through the device two more passes, about one iteration more (section 2.4). RaDe-GS writes the squared form (row 632); Gaussian Opacity Fields the absolute form, on its intersection depths (row 633). The squared form is section 2.4's row 7: cells 7a, 7d and 7e supply it from two channels, the sum over ordered pairs being half of row 7's double sum and the NDC map a function of z applied before compositing (this ticket's reading). The absolute form is row 6: cells 6a and 6d say no for E6's render, cell 6c yes for flat primitives, and the pair list is unmeasured (the owner's open question). All three papers take the depth where the ray meets the primitive, per pixel; with E6's centre depth the term measures the spread of the centres along the ray | Orientation and scale do not enter. It acts on `means` through the depths and on the other slice tensors through the weights. A spec revision would have to choose, the more so as E8 already lowers opacities. The shell is weight spread along a ray, which is what the term penalizes; but it sees only the primitives alive at the training image's time (E3, E4), so two shell primitives of different times never meet in it (this ticket's reading). The device composites values at or above 0, or coded affinely (cell 8d), so a signed plane distance needs a code there, as the normal has; the colour pass takes it as it is. It needs no median. Default off leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests | The outputs of the row above, for two cameras: section 2.4, cells 4a and 8a, twice. The photometric term also reads the two training images. Constant orientation, E1, short durations, relocation and split: nothing beyond the rows above | **This is the row that assumes the cameras agree.** The geometric term checks the model's own geometry between two renders, and the images do not enter it. PGSR's own limit is regions seen from few viewpoints (row 631). Family E handed MVGSR's multi-view consistency loss to this family: its abstract says the loss is applied beside distractor masks built from multi-view feature consistency (row 640); it was not read in full, the cap being reached | none: no prior map; the neighbour's training image (row 631) | PGSR, Tanks and Temples (Table IV; row 631): without the multi-view terms the F-score is 0.32 against 0.52, the largest effect in the table, while PSNR is higher without them, 27.30 against 26.73. Without the geometric term 0.49, without the photometric term 0.39. Without the weight that drops occluded pixels, 0.28 and 21.70 dB: the term harms when it is applied where the two views do not match. Afterwards nothing holds a primitive flat or turned: GeoGaussian pins the thin scale, CoMVS-GS keeps the flattening loss from the first iteration. That term is section 3.4's (row 629) | Paper only: the repository carries no license (row 639) | **A change to a quantity, with new rasterization kernels for its depth.** The third scale is fixed at 0, so E5's covariance has rank 2; colour is splatted by the usual affine projection of that covariance; depth per pixel and primitive is the centre's depth plus a slope term from a Taylor expansion of the ray–ellipse intersection (Eqs. 2, 7; row 629). `log_scales` cannot hold a zero, §1.1 and E5 change, and neither E6's kernel nor gsplat 1.5.3's 2DGS kernel computes that depth. The slope term is linear in the pixel's offset from the centre, with coefficients fixed per primitive and camera, so its blend is in principle three composited channels (cell 8a) and not a kernel change (this ticket's algebra; not run, and the paper computes it in its rasterizer) | The normal is the rotation's third column (row 629). With a zero scale the rendering loss has no gradient on that column, the paper shows, so the depth–normal consistency loss becomes necessary and its gradient on the normal is scaled by 10. The paper starts from random positions and rotations inside the object's bounding box and found SfM points no better, which bears on D-5's identity rotation: it is evidence that such a method can orient primitives from nothing, on masked objects. It prunes points that received no gradient over one pass of the images. E11's scale formula with a zero scale, and E1, are outside the paper | Its scenes are single objects with foreground masks, and a mask loss on the rendered alpha is one of its five terms; §2.2 allows no mask on a training image. Few cameras: trained on half of DTU's images it keeps 31.70 dB against 30.08 for 3DGS (Table 3). Its limits: strong specular reflection, and a global shift of the surface where texture is weak (row 629) | For the method as published: a normal map per training image (section 3.4) and a foreground mask per training image. Maps the user supplies would do for both | DTU, Chamfer distance and PSNR, one term removed at a time (Table 4; row 629): full 0.882 and 32.51; without depth–normal consistency 1.243 and 31.56; without the normal prior 1.070 and 32.63; without the opacity term 1.085 and 32.08; without masks 1.015 and 29.10. Whole method 0.88 against 2.58 for 3DGS by its own evaluation | 6.67 minutes on DTU against 5.19 for 3DGS, 15,000 iterations on an RTX 4090 (row 629) |
| **Rasterized depth and normals for unflattened 3D Gaussians** (RaDe-GS, Zhang et al., 2024, arXiv 2406.01467) | none: no estimator (row 632) | Paper only: the non-commercial Gaussian-Splatting License (row 638) | **New rasterization kernels**, as published: custom CUDA kernels on the 3DGS code compute a depth per pixel and primitive and a normal per primitive and camera, and the two losses of the rows on depth–normal consistency and on depth distortion read them. The primitive, E5 and the colour of E6 stay. gsplat 1.5.3 has no such kernel, so under decision 1's third edge it is recorded and cannot be a finalist as published. One reading would move it to a loss that needs new render outputs: next column (row 632) | The depth is the centre's depth plus a term linear in the pixel's offset, with coefficients fixed per primitive and camera (Eq. 4), and the normal is one vector per primitive and camera (Eqs. 27, 28; row 632). A blend of such a depth is three composited channels and the normal three more, which section 2.4's cells 8a and 4a supply from values computed in PyTorch (this ticket's algebra; not run). The squared distortion on per-pixel depths would need the products as further channels. Nothing here was checked against the paper's kernels, and the reference backend would need the same values | **It asks the least of today's primitive** (this ticket's reading): nothing is flattened and no axis is named. The normal is that of the plane through the points where each ray meets the Gaussian's largest value, under E6's own affine projection (row 632); it is no axis of the primitive. For D-5's spheres that plane faces the camera, so the terms say little until primitives are anisotropic (this ticket's reading); the paper trains 15,000 iterations before turning them on. Constant orientation and scale, E1, relocation and split: no conflict, since the stored quantities are today's | Single-view outputs and losses. Mip-NeRF 360: outdoors 25.17 dB against 24.64 for 3DGS and 24.34 for 2DGS, so no loss of image quality against 3DGS there. **No ablation removes a part** (row 632) | On DTU 8.3 minutes against 8.9 for 2DGS and 55 for Gaussian Opacity Fields, by its own table; the added time is the pixel-wise terms, forward and backward (row 632) |
| **Ray–Gaussian intersection rendering, with the intersection plane's normal** (Gaussian Opacity Fields, Yu et al., 2024, arXiv 2404.10772) | none: no estimator (row 633) | Paper only: the non-commercial Gaussian-Splatting License (row 637) | **New rasterization kernels.** Each primitive's contribution to a pixel is evaluated where the 3D Gaussian is largest along the ray, in custom CUDA kernels, in place of E6's projected Gaussian; the opacity field this allows serves meshing after training (row 633). gsplat 1.5.3 has no such kernel: recorded, and not a finalist under decision 1's third edge. Its two losses and its growth statistic have their own rows | A depth per ray and primitive at the intersection, and a normal per ray and primitive: neither is a value fixed per primitive, so section 2.4's cells 4a and 8a do not supply them (this ticket's reading) | The primitive stays a 3D Gaussian with today's quantities. **Estimators first met in this family.**

No row. The one estimator this family's rows name is Omnidata's normal model, which Gaussian Surfels' normal-prior term reads (row 629); section 3.3 holds its row, and its verdict is fail, the weights being non-commercial. No other technique screened here uses a pretrained network. Where a paper met in the rounds adds one, its abstract names the kind and not the model (monocular normals in SolidGS and GausSurf, monocular depth in GVGS and 2D-SuGaR, learned multi-view stereo in Sparse2DGS, segmentation and normal models in GSPlane; rows 640, 642, 643), and that part is handed to section 3.4. The multi-view stereo of Kim et al. and of CoMVS-GS is classical, with no weights (rows 650, 646).

**The counts.** 96 results seen; 32 abstracts read, of the cap's 60; 12 papers read in full, the cap's 12; 17 rows. Three further web searches looked only for the repository addresses of RaDe-GS and Gaussian Surfels, which their papers do not give, and screened nothing. Two of the twelve full reads, SharpTimeGS and 4DSurf, were reads of the parts that bear on this family's losses and of nothing else (rows 647, 648).

**The abstract screen** was fixed before round 1. A title passes when it suggests a method that constrains the shape, the orientation or the depth of Gaussian primitives to a surface; its abstract is then read and counted. An abstract passes when it describes such a technique acting on the primitives during training, not only a meshing step after it; when the technique is new to the survey, or differs from a row in a way that would change column 3, 4 or 5; and when its main ingredient is not a prior map, an opacity regularizer, a dynamic-scene mechanism or robustness to inconsistent images, in which case it is named with its family and does not count as a pass.

**The search seeds.** Each of the six has rows; none is left without one.

- **2D Gaussian Splatting:** its primitive is the row on the flat disk; its two losses are the rows on depth–normal consistency and on depth distortion.
- **Gaussian Surfels:** its primitive and depth are the row on the surfel; its depth–normal term is in the row on depth–normal consistency. Its normal-prior term goes to section 3.4 and its opacity term to section 3.2.
- **SuGaR:** the row on its regularization. Its entropy loss on opacities goes to section 3.2; its meshing and its binding of Gaussians to the mesh were not screened, being steps after training.
- **PGSR:** the rows on the flattening loss, on plane-distance depth and on multi-view consistency; its single-view loss is in the row on depth–normal consistency. Its exposure model goes to section 3.6.
- **RaDe-GS:** the row on rasterized depth and normals; its two losses are 2DGS's.
- **Gaussian Opacity Fields:** the row on ray–Gaussian intersection rendering and the row on the growth statistic; its two losses are in the rows on depth–normal consistency and on depth distortion. Its appearance network goes to section 3.6.

**The recency round** is rounds 7 and 8: an arXiv search of abstracts submitted from 2025-10-01, and a web search naming FreeTimeGS for the papers that cite it. Round 7 gave eleven results, of which CoMVS-GS (2026-08) was read in full and has rows; VA-GS (2025-10) and DepthGS (2026-11) passed and were not read. Round 8 gave 2DGS-R (2025-10), passed and not read, and three papers that cite or build on FreeTimeGS or treat dynamic surfaces, left to section 3.5 by title. The two names family D handed over, SharpTimeGS (2026-02) and 4DSurf (2026-03, revised 2026-10), are of the same period. Four techniques that passed in earlier rounds also date from it: SparseSurf (2025-11), TriaGS (2025-12), GVGS (2026-01) and Gaussian Wrapping (2026-04). What the period adds to the rows is the oriented initialization and the stereo supervision of CoMVS-GS, and the dynamic methods' use of the older losses; no technique of the period that was read changes what a loss on today's primitives can be.

**How the family reached its stop rule.** By the cap. The twelfth full read was reached with 32 of the 60 abstracts used, and no row can be added without a read. The other condition, two consecutive rounds that add no technique passing the abstract screen, was not met: round 4 added none, and every other round added at least one. So the family is not exhausted, and the list below is what the cap left unread. The owner can move the caps.

**Passed the abstract screen and not read in full**, the cap being reached; each is known here by its abstract alone, and none has a row.

- **SparseSurf** (arXiv 2511.14633): for sparse views, a feature-based geometric consistency over training and unseen views beside a stereo alignment of geometry and texture; its abstract is also the source of the caution on flattened Gaussians in the row on the flattening loss (row 642).
- **TriaGS** (arXiv 2512.06269): a rendered 3D point is penalized for its distance to a consensus point triangulated again from a bundle of neighbouring views. A multi-view term that needs several renders per iteration (row 640).
- **GVGS** (arXiv 2601.20331): visibility is modelled per primitive and aggregated across views for the multi-view term, in place of a depth reprojection test; `RenderOutput`'s `visible` is the kind of input it would read. Its monocular depth alignment is section 3.4's (row 640).
- **VA-GS** (arXiv 2510.11473) and **DepthGS** (arXiv 2611.13628): variants of the rows on depth–normal and multi-view consistency, with edge cues and deep image features, and with a reliability weight on the normal term from local depth differences (row 643).
- **GausSurf** (arXiv 2411.19454): patch-match stereo guides geometry in an iterative scheme, close to CoMVS-GS's row; its normal priors are section 3.4's (row 640).
- **Gaussian Wrapping** (arXiv 2604.07337): a learnable oriented normal per Gaussian, which would be a new quantity, with a changed rasterizer (row 641).
- **GeoSplat** (arXiv 2509.05075): initial scales from principal curvatures estimated on the points, and curvature-aware growth; a relative of the row on oriented initialization (row 641).
- **TopoSurfel** (arXiv 2608.20687): a proxy mesh extracted during training guides the surfels' normals and their growth (row 640).
- **2DGS-R** (arXiv 2510.16837): 2DGS trained with its normal term, then cloned in place where it renders poorly and fine-tuned with opacity frozen; its abstract holds that geometry and rendering quality were not both reached in one stage (row 644).
- **Geometry Field Splatting with Gaussian Surfels** (arXiv 2411.17067) and **SolidGS** (arXiv 2412.15400): each changes the kernel, the first to splat a geometry field, the second to a more solid kernel for sparse views; new rasterization kernels by their abstracts (row 640).

**Handed to other families.** Each is named where it was met and left to its section.

- **To section 3.4 (prior maps):** the normal-prior loss of Gaussian Surfels; the monocular normals of SolidGS and GausSurf; GVGS's alignment of monocular depth; SparseSurf's stereo depth, if it comes from a network, as a search result's summary and not its abstract says; Sparse2DGS's initialization from learned multi-view stereo; 2D-SuGaR; GSPlane; DRGSplat and DN-Splatter, met in search results only; arXiv 2607.03765, by its title (rows 629, 640, 642, 643).
- **To section 3.2 (opacity and floaters):** the opacity term of Gaussian Surfels, which pushes each opacity to 0 or 1; SuGaR's entropy loss on opacities and its removal of primitives under 0.5; StableGS, by its title; the pruning measure MVGSR's abstract describes. TrimGS's trimming has its row here and also bears on that family (rows 629, 630, 640, 649).
- **To section 3.5 (dynamic surfaces):** arXiv 2411.06602, Vidu4D, DeSiRe-GS, SPAGS, REArtGS++, FreeTimeGS++, GauSTAR and DynaSurfGS, by their titles; SharpTimeGS's lifespan and 4DSurf's regularization of the flow of a signed distance function, which are that family's own (rows 643, 647, 648).
- **To section 3.6 (cameras that disagree):** MVGSR's distractor masks from multi-view feature consistency; PGSR's exposure coefficients per frame; the appearance network Gaussian Opacity Fields and RaDe-GS take from VastGaussian; VA-GS's consistency of deep image features between views. And one name met by accident: the page of arXiv 2603.02893 shows the abstract of a work that reconstructs a person from images generated by a multi-view diffusion model, with the cameras' poses optimized jointly (it calls itself MVD-HuGaS); nothing but the name was taken (rows 640, 631, 633, 632, 643, 642).

**Handed to this family**, as names to check by its own reads.

- **From family D:** SharpTimeGS's PGSR-style flattening and its consistency between normals and depth, and 4DSurf's use of 2DGS's two losses. Both were read for those parts: each keeps the terms as standing regularizers, and neither ablation removes them (rows 647, 648). Vidu4D's normal consistency was checked on its abstract alone: its geometric regularization is applied in the warped state, with normals estimated from its continuous warping fields, so it is tied to a deformation today's primitives do not have (row 654). Its method was not read, the cap being reached. The abstract also says it reconstructs from a single generated video, which sections 3.5 and 3.6 may want.
- **From family E:** MVGSR's multi-view consistency loss. Not read in full, the cap being reached; its abstract says only that such a loss is applied (row 640).

**What the rows show, for review 1.** Input to section 4, which chooses.

- **Two rows are losses on today's primitives:** the flattening loss and GeoGaussian's co-planarity loss. Neither is isolated by an ablation in any paper this section read; section 3.4's read of VCR-GauS isolates a scale loss of the flattening kind, F-score 0.36 without it and 0.40 with it (row 726). The flattening loss is nonetheless the term every planar method read keeps, static or dynamic.
- **The term with the evidence is depth–normal consistency:** five papers isolate it, and the three that measure it on DTU find Chamfer distances about 30 % lower with it. gsplat 1.5.3's stage functions and the reference backend can supply an expected-depth form of it for today's primitives (section 2.4). Two things stand between that form and the published ones. The published normal of an unflattened 3D Gaussian that needs no kernel is the smallest-scale direction, which Gaussian Opacity Fields measured as worse than a kernel-made normal and which is undefined for a sphere. And two of the papers take the normal from median depth, which E6's render does not supply; one paper found mean depth clearly worse, in another loss.
- **PGSR's formulation is the one that asks nothing of the kernels:** a flattening loss on the scales, then normals and plane distances that are fixed per primitive and camera and can be composited as channels. This is this ticket's reading of its equations, and it was not run.
- **Where the orientation comes from.** The methods read either start from oriented points (GeoGaussian, CoMVS-GS) or leave the orientation to training. Several hold their geometry terms back, from 2,000 iterations (GeoGaussian) to 15,000 (RaDe-GS), or raise them from 0 (Gaussian Surfels); 2DGS, PGSR and Gaussian Opacity Fields state no delay in the lines read. Today's primitives start as spheres at the identity rotation, and no init point carries a normal.
- **Constant orientation.** Every technique with a row here comes from a static method, where the question does not arise. Of the two dynamic methods read in part, 4DSurf moves 2DGS's disks from a canonical shape by a velocity field; whether it or SharpTimeGS turns a primitive in time was not in the lines read, and section 3.5 screens that. With durations of one to four frames (section 2.6) an orientation set per primitive is right for most of its life; the question that remains is whether training can set it.
- **Cameras that disagree.** The single-view terms assume nothing between cameras and so cannot resolve a disagreement: they can make the shell flat, thin and aligned with its own depth. The multi-view terms assume agreement to within about a pixel, and the person's matches on private scene A are 1.84 px apart at the median (section 2.5). PGSR's own ablation shows its multi-view term doing harm when it is applied to pixels where two views do not match. PGSR and Gaussian Opacity Fields absorb a difference of illumination between images in an appearance model, and each reports a better F-score with it than without, 0.52 against 0.49 and 0.46 against 0.43.
- **Geometry terms usually cost image quality.** 2DGS, PGSR, TrimGS and CoMVS-GS each say so, by 0.3 to 0.6 dB where a figure was read. Gaussian Surfels' consistency term and RaDe-GS go the other way.

### 3.2 Family B: opacity and floater regularizers

*Ticket #8's section.* The survey of family B, with the cap of 60 abstracts and 12 full reads; a technique that only moves a value the notes already tried (section 2.1) is recorded as tried, not screened.

**Screening table.**

| Technique | 1. Weights' license | 2. Code license | 3. Integration class | 4. Render backends | 5. Fit with the primitives | 6. Cameras that disagree | 7. Prior maps | 8. Evidence | 9. Training cost |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Neural Decaying Function** (4C4D; Zhou et al., CVPR 2026, arXiv 2604.04063; the owner's search seed). A small network, trained with the scene, maps a primitive's centre, opacity and rotation to a factor τ on the opacity it is rendered with; primitives outside the current view or time take a constant 0.999 instead | None for the technique: the network is trained with the scene, from nothing (row 655). The paper's pipeline initializes from MASt3R, without naming a file; the file section 3.3 screens **fails** the bar (section 3.3, MASt3R's row). Today's initialization (D-58) stands in its place | MIT by the repository's license file, whose notice is that of the 4D Gaussian Splatting the paper builds on; no other file was opened, so parts under another license are not ruled out (rows 656, 657). Taken from the paper only | **A change to a quantity or to motion:** E3's opacity gets a learned factor, σ_eff = τ·σt(t)·σ, and a network joins the trained state. The field's default (no network, τ = 1) leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched. Not stated, and both matter to the exchange model (§2.2): whether τ stays at test time, and whether the 0.999 multiplies the stored opacity (row 655) | Nothing beyond E6's render: τ multiplies the opacity the renderer receives (E3). The visibility test needs which primitives the view holds, which `RenderOutput.visible` gives with `return_aux` (section 2.3), and whether the duration covers the timestamp, which the stored tensors give (row 655) | It acts on the base opacity, not on the duration: the network reads neither the time centre nor the duration, and adds no gradient to `log_durations` (row 655). The rotation it reads is constant per primitive and starts at the identity (D-5); the centre would be `means` or E1's moved position, which the paper's base model does not separate. The constant 0.999 can matter only if it multiplies the stored opacity, since a primitive outside the view or the time is not rendered: this ticket's reading, as in the constant-decay row below. Read so, it is a decay at every iteration that the rendering loss must outweigh, and it falls hardest on primitives that are seldom in view and in time. A primitive of two frames' duration has a temporal factor that averages 0.04 over private scene A's 120 frames (E4), so the decay presses the under-two-frames population near the init points before the longer-lived one far from them (section 2.6). The learned τ cannot tell those two apart by opacity: their medians are 0.18 and 0.12 (section 2.6). D-14 reads the stored opacity: what the decay brings under 0.005 is relocated by E10 and E11 until iteration 25,000 and stays dead after, on top of the 7 to 17 % already dead at 30,000 (section 2.6); what τ alone switches off stays live for D-14 and for E10's ρ unless they read τ·σ. E8 keeps acting on σ. A split's children (E17, D-43) and E11's copies take their τ at their own positions | It assumes real, synchronized cameras with calibrated poses: its own captures are re-aligned in time and posed with COLMAP (row 655). Few cameras are its subject: four training cameras out of 18 to 24 on Neural3DV, ENeRF-Outdoor and Mobile-Stage, the others held out. Its diagnosis is that with few cameras the training views are fitted while the geometry stays poor, which it shows in depth pictures. No experiment with images that disagree or with pixels known to be wrong | None for the function. The paper's initialization from MASt3R is one from point maps, section 3.4's matter (row 655) | Neural3DV at four cameras, PSNR on the held-out cameras, on one scene it calls representative (its Tables 4 and 5): no decay 22.60 dB; a constant decay of 0.9, as Binocular3DGS's, 24.31; hand-made power and exponential functions of the opacity 24.35 and 24.32; the learned function without the visibility split 24.49, and with it 24.68. So a constant decay brings 1.7 of the 2.1 dB, and learning it the rest. Over the six scenes (Table 1): 22.29 dB against 20.82 for the best of four baselines. Geometry is shown in depth pictures only, with no depth or surface metric, and no limitation is stated (row 655) | Not stated: neither the network's size nor the training time (row 655). A field for it defaults to off and leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched (the technique: row 658) | None: it reads the stored opacity logits only, as `reg4d_loss(model, t)` does (section 2.3) (the technique: row 658) | Base opacity only; no gradient reaches `log_durations`, as under E8. What differs from E8 is who is pressed. E8's gradient carries σt(t), so over timestamps drawn evenly a primitive of duration s in a clip of length T receives about √(2π)·s/T of what this form gives it: 0.04 for two frames in private scene A's 120, and up to 1 for a primitive that lasts the clip (this ticket's arithmetic on E4 and E8). At equal weight it therefore presses the under-two-frames population about 24 times harder than E8 does and the long-lived primitives as E8 does, where the notes' 10 to 100 times `loss.lambda_reg` pressed all alike and went from mixed to collapse (section 2.1). It still cannot tell the shell from the surface by σ: the medians far from and near the init points are 0.12 and 0.18 (section 2.6). What it brings under D-14's 0.005 is relocated by E10 and E11 until iteration 25,000 and stays dead after; `loss.lambda_reg` 0.1 already left 15 to 17 % dead at 30,000, against 7 % (section 2.6). E11 lowers a target's opacity to 1 − (1 − σ)^(1/n), and the penalty then lowers it further, as E8 does. No experiment with few cameras, with images that disagree or with pixels known to be wrong (row 658) | None (row 658) | MipNeRF 360 from a random initialization, PSNR (its Table 3): the method 29.72 dB, without its two penalties 23.90; 3DGS 27.89, and 23.84 with the two penalties added, which the paper puts down to a clash with 3DGS's density control. The two penalties are removed and added together, so nothing isolates this one. A field for it defaults to off and leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched (the technique: row 658) | None: stored tensors only (row 658) | Neither the base opacity nor the duration. It shrinks every primitive, the large ones most, since the penalty is the scale itself. Scales are constant in time (§1.1), so the term is the same at every timestamp and needs no temporal factor. It reads no orientation and no ratio between axes, so it neither flattens a primitive nor keeps it round. Section 2.6 measured no scale, so which of its populations hold the large primitives is not known. E11 already shrinks a target and its copies, and E17 divides a split's scales by 1.6; the penalty pushes the same way. So it acts on neither of them. While relocation runs almost nothing is under the threshold (at most 0.02 % of the primitives, section 2.6), and E11 floors a moved copy's opacity at it, so the noise would reach only the copies that land at or near the floor, those of faint targets or of targets shared many ways; after iteration 25,000 it would move the 7 to 17 % left dead, at a position learning rate that has decayed a hundredfold by the end (§1.3 "Optimizer and learning rates"). The offset is shaped by the covariance, which is constant in time, and is spatial only: nothing explores the time centre or the duration | Static scenes from real cameras that agree. Against E8 it differs twice. E8 presses a primitive in proportion to σt(t), so only near its time centre, and through σ(1 − σ), so less and less as σ falls; the decay takes the same share from every primitive at every iteration, whatever its time and its opacity. The paper's 0.995 was set on static scenes from 3 to 8 views, where a primitive in view is updated at most iterations. Here the rendering loss supports a primitive in proportion to its temporal factor, which for a duration of two frames averages 0.04 over private scene A's 120 frames and is above 0.61 over only four frame intervals (E4; section 2.6), while the decay acts at every iteration at full strength: at the paper's rate it takes 11 % of an opacity in 24 iterations and half in 138 (this ticket's arithmetic). So the decay falls first on the under-two-frames population near the init points, and less on the longer-lived primitives far from them (section 2.6); a rate would have to be set against the share of the clip a primitive is active in. A primitive nothing supports goes from the median 0.16 to D-14's 0.005 in about 700 iterations. What falls under 0.005 is relocated by E10 and E11 until iteration 25,000 and stays dead after, adding to the 7 to 17 % already dead at 30,000 (section 2.6). E11 gives a moved copy its target's share, 1 − (1 − σ)^(1/n), floored at 0.005; a copy at that floor, from a faint target or one shared many ways, is put under it again by one decay step and is dead at the next event unless the rendering loss lifts it.
