**The counts.** 212 results seen: 120 in the eleven web rounds and the look-up of one seed, the 72 titles of round 12, and 20 in two searches by name. 48 abstracts read, of the cap's 60; 12 papers read in full, the cap's 12; 15 rows. The license files of seven of those papers' repositories were looked for, and the terms of four estimators (rows 720, 722, 724, 727, 729, 740, 741; rows 736, 737, 738, 739).

**The abstract screen,** as applied from round 1. A title passes when it suggests a loss or an initialization that reads a map predicted by a pretrained network; its abstract is then read and counted. An abstract passes when it describes such a technique for a per-scene optimization, not a feed-forward network or an editing pipeline, and when the technique is new to the survey or differs from one already passed in the form of its loss, in the prior map it reads or in what it initializes. A technique whose main ingredient is another family's is named with that family and does not count as a pass.

**The search seeds.** Each has a row.

- **DN-Splatter:** three rows, the aligned depth loss, the normal loss and the dense initialization with orientations. Its loss on the smallest scale is family A's flattening loss (section 3.1).
- **DNGaussian:** the row on hard and soft depth regularization.
- **Initialization from the DUSt3R and VGGT line:** the rows of RI3D and MonoFusion, both of which start from DUSt3R's point maps, with the passing estimators that could stand in. Starts from VGGT passed the abstract screen three times (Gesplat, Fast 3D Foundation Model Initialized Gaussian Splatting, Ground4D) and none was read in full; 4C4D starts from MASt3R (row 655). DUSt3R, MASt3R and VGGT all fail the bar (section 3.3).
- **MotionCrafter:** the last row. The estimator fails the bar, and no paper read initializes a reconstruction from its outputs, so the row is this ticket's reading of the estimator's paper against §1.3, beside the two nearest measured techniques, the rows on points added from depth and scene flow and on lifted tracks.

**The recency round** is rounds 3 and 12: a web search that names FreeTimeGS, and the titles of the 72 papers that cite it, 69 of them dated 2025-10 or later (row 661). Five of the twelve papers read in full are of 2025-10 or later: GC-4DGS (2025-11), MOSAIC-GS (2026-01), MotionCrafter (2026-02), MoRGS (2026-03) and In Depth We Trust (2026-04). Of the papers that cite FreeTimeGS, MotionCrafter has its row; UniFusion and Sparse Dynamic Cameras are known by their abstracts; RetimeGS and SharpTimeGS by section 3.2's reads (rows 668, 689); and FreeTimeGS++ by its abstract only, its full text unread under the interim rule on papers that reproduce FreeTimeGS (rows 711, 663). No cell of this section rests on an authors' reproduction of FreeTimeGS, and none of the twelve papers read describes one.

**How the family reached its stop rule.** By the cap of full reads: the twelfth was reached with 48 of the 60 abstracts used. The other condition was not met. Rounds 10 and 12 added no technique that passed the abstract screen, but they were not consecutive, and round 11 added three: the search had not run dry, and a larger cap would add rows. The owner can move the caps.

**Passed the abstract screen and not read in full**, the cap being reached; each is known here by its abstract alone, and none has a row.

- **UniFusion** (arXiv 2609.05888): monocular depth maps across views and time represented as spatio-temporal neural fields and aligned jointly, without separating foreground from background and without masks or tracks, with a multi-view depth-order loss; the aligned depths initialize and supervise a 4D reconstruction from few cameras. By its abstract it is aimed at what section 2.7 found, that one scale and one shift per image do not hold the person and the background together; it cites FreeTimeGS (rows 742, 661).
- **D²-4DGS** (arXiv 2608.01588): for few cameras, monocular depth aligned with multi-view geometric depth and checked against it, the verified anchors driving pruning, depth supervision and densification; the class of GC-4DGS's two rows (row 711).
- **4D Reconstruction from Sparse Dynamic Cameras** (arXiv 2606.04593): 3D tracks initialized by joining feature matches between cameras with point tracks within a camera, and a depth-ordering loss it calls robust to noise; relatives of the rows on lifted tracks and on ranking (row 742).
- **Sparse-View 4D Gaussian Splatting via Spatiotemporal Priors and Generative Assistance** (arXiv 2610.04606): a challenge entry for six cameras; masks guide the initialization and the densification, monocular depth aligned to metric scale regularizes the background only, and optical flow constrains the projected motion (row 742).
- **MonoInstance** (arXiv 2503.18363): the depths of each segmented instance from several views are aligned in one 3D space, the density of the resulting points gives an uncertainty for the depth prior, and a mask term takes over where it is high (row 718).
- **Shape of Motion** (arXiv 2407.13764): monocular depth maps and long 2D tracks consolidated into one dynamic scene; MOSAIC-GS and MonoFusion describe and build on its lifted tracks (rows 713, 728, 730).
- **GaussianFlow** (arXiv 2403.12365) and **Motion-aware 3DGS** (arXiv 2403.11447): the flow loss of MoRGS's row in two earlier forms, the second with an uncertainty on the flow (row 714).
- **MVPGS** (arXiv 2409.14316) and **FatesGS** (arXiv 2501.04628): ranking losses on monocular depth for few views of static scenes, the first beside learned multi-view stereo (row 715).
- **FSGS** (arXiv 2312.00451), **HDGS** (arXiv 2505.22279), **LoopSparseGS** (arXiv 2408.00254) and **Depth Aware 3DGS** (arXiv 2410.11080): monocular depth losses for few views, by correlation at one or several scales, by alignment to sparse depth in windows, and by a scale-invariant form (rows 709, 711).
- **Gesplat** (arXiv 2510.10097), **Fast 3D Foundation Model Initialized Gaussian Splatting** (arXiv 2607.03209), **Ground4D** (arXiv 2606.28828) and **Pi-GS** (arXiv 2602.03327): starts from the point maps of VGGT or π³, the last with an uncertainty-guided depth loss (rows 712, 717, 718).
- **GaussianRoom** (arXiv 2405.19671) and **Endo-4DGS** (arXiv 2401.16416): a normal-prior term for textureless rooms, and a confidence-guided depth loss for endoscopic video (rows 710, 717).

**Handed to other families.** Each is named where it was met and left to its section.

- **To section 3.1 (surface-following primitives):** the loss on the smallest scale that DN-Splatter and VCR-GauS both add; VCR-GauS's densification of large surface primitives and its split along the largest axis; GaussianRoom's signed distance field that guides densification; G3Splat and TriaGS, by abstract and title; StableGS's self-supervised depth consistency; and the uncertainty-weighted smoothing of rendered depth in 4D Gaussian Splatting in the Wild, which reads no prior map (rows 719, 726, 710, 712, 718, 731).
- **To section 3.2 (opacity and floaters):** StableGS's two opacities, whose row is there; SparseGS's pruning and its mode depth as a measure of floaters; and a paper on false transparency, arXiv 2510.15736, by its title (rows 712, 735).
- **To section 3.5 (dynamic surfaces):** MonoFusion's motion bases and rigidity loss; MOSAIC-GS's rigid refinement per object, its Poly-Fourier trajectories and its split into static and dynamic primitives; MoRGS's offset field and motion confidence; Shape of Motion's motion bases; Splatography, 4DSurf, AT-GS, the compact representation of arXiv 2311.12897 and the motion bases of arXiv 2603.25058; FreeTimeGS++, by its abstract only; and, by their titles in round 12, arXiv 2603.00952, 2602.03538, 2603.24994, 2604.00538, 2605.23672 and 2611.04666 (rows 728, 730, 733, 713, 711, 714, 743).
- **To section 3.6 (cameras that disagree):** SparseCam4D, which models generated observations that are inconsistent with a distortion field over space and time; the diffusion models of RI3D, of the challenge entry and of 4D Gaussian Splatting in the Wild; the joint refinement of poses in Gesplat and in Fast 3D Foundation Model Initialized Gaussian Splatting; USplat4D, by its title; and, by their titles in round 12, two papers on multi-view video diffusion for people, arXiv 2606.13655 and 2510.07190. GC-4DGS's consistency check and VCR-GauS's confidence weight are that family's idea of weighting by trust, applied to a prior map; their rows stay here (rows 711, 734, 742, 731, 712, 743).
- **Body templates, recorded and not searched for** (decision 1): the silhouette losses of OccGaussian and SplatArmor, which round 10's results describe, and the keypoint fitting of arXiv 2607.09125 (row 743). A loss between rendered alpha and a foreground mask was met only inside such methods and has no row.

**Handed to this family**, as names to check by its own reads.

- **From family A:** the normal-prior loss of Gaussian Surfels, the monocular normals of SolidGS and GausSurf, GVGS's alignment of monocular depth, SparseSurf's stereo depth, Sparse2DGS's start from learned multi-view stereo, 2D-SuGaR, GSPlane, DRGSplat and arXiv 2607.03765. None was read, the cap being reached. Their classes have rows: the normal loss, the aligned depth loss and the stereo depth.
- **From family B:** SparseGS was read and has its row. 4C4D's start from MASt3R is in section 3.2's read, which records no ablation of it (row 655). StableGS's prior from DUSt3R's point maps is in section 3.2's read too: on Mip-NeRF 360 with all views it changes PSNR by 0.01 dB (31.54 and 26.74 with it, 31.55 and 26.73 without) (row 702). SharpTimeGS, a 4D method that cites FreeTimeGS, initializes from RAFT flow, SAM 2 masks and COLMAP points (row 689), and RetimeGS's abstract names an initialization and a supervision from optical flow (row 668): both are the nearest relatives of the rows on flow and on lifted tracks, and neither was read here. StereoGS and TIDI-GS were not read (rows 682, 674).
- **From families D and E:** MonoFusion and GC-4DGS were read and have rows here; MonoFusion's row covers its depth alignment and its start, and its motion model is left to section 3.5. UniFusion, D²-4DGS, Sparse Dynamic Cameras and the challenge entry are in the list above, by their abstracts. AniGS's normal loss is cited in the normal loss's row from section 3.2's read (row 703). SPIn-NeRF's abstract names no depth term, and this section did not read the paper (row 742); section 3.6's full read records that the split loss carries a depth term, without its form (row 811), so the term has no row here. The hand-over's last name, an initial velocity from optical flow in FreeTimeGS++, could not be checked: that paper's full text is unread under the interim rule, so this section records nothing of it beyond its abstract (row 711), and its estimator was not screened here.

**Estimators met and not screened.** Kosmo-GFM (arXiv 2611.16840, of 2026-11-12) predicts, by its abstract, depth, cameras, correspondences, dynamic 3D points and scene flow, which would make it an estimator of the one kind where section 3.3 found none that passes; its project page returned no text, and no weights page or license statement of it was found (row 743). π³, which Pi-GS starts from, and UFM, which FreeTimeGS++'s abstract names, were not looked up. Each would be screened in section 3.3's way before any row leans on it.

**What the rows show, for review 1.** Input to section 4, which chooses.

- **Every loss here can be rendered today.** Each row's loss reads expected depth, composited normals, normals taken from the depth map, or a composited 2D vector, all of which section 2.4 supplies as channels of the colour pass or through the device. None needs the median or the pairwise distortion, so none raises the pair-list question. Two forms fall outside: SparseGS's softmax and mode depths, which need each pixel's individual weights, and VCR-GauS's intersection depth, which needs new kernels or the 2DGS ones; both papers measure what is lost without them.
- **Column 6 sorts the losses into four groups, and section 2.7 says what each meets on private scene A.** Losses taken within one image (the Pearson loss, the ranking loss, DNGaussian's normalized depth, the gradient term, the normal loss) fit no scale, shift or anchor, so the 4% by which the pairs' anchors disagree does not reach them; by the same invariance they shape a surface and cannot place it, so none of them can by itself bring a layer of faint primitives back onto the body. Losses that pass through an alignment (the aligned depth loss, MonoFusion's depth target, the absolute term of In Depth We Trust) do place the surface, at the depth of whatever they were aligned to, and inherit the misfit of one scale and shift per image and the pairs' disagreement. One technique compares the cameras and drops what disagrees (GC-4DGS's check), which on this scene would leave it silent on the person where it is needed. Two use the model's own renders to decide where a prior acts or how much (the mask of In Depth We Trust, VCR-GauS's confidence weight).
- **The measured gains come from few views and shrink as views are added.** DNGaussian gains 2.7 dB with 3 views and nothing with 9; In Depth We Trust gains 0.47 dB with 16 to 39 views and 0.13 dB with 48 to 117, and measures the plain aligned loss at no gain or a loss; DN-Splatter's monocular depth helps a mesh and leaves PSNR where it was. Those papers add a depth prior where the colours under-constrain the geometry. On private scene A nine cameras constrain it and contradict one another, a case no paper read tests. So for depth priors H1 rests on thinner evidence from papers than the row count suggests, and nothing read speaks against H2.
- **Three terms are aimed at the shell's mechanism,** by this ticket's reading of their papers: primitives that answer a constraint by turning faint instead of moving. They are DNGaussian's hard depth, which counts every primitive as nearly opaque; the mask of In Depth We Trust, which finds the pixels whose rendered depth changes with the viewpoint; and SparseGS's softmax depth, which the gsplat backend cannot supply. The first two are losses that need new render outputs and nothing else.
- **Initialization from files needs no spec change for positions and velocities.** `ftgs-init` v1 already carries both, and supplied points skip steps 1 to 6 (§1.3), so a producer outside the package can be tried against today's code, which no loss here can. What a start from prior maps adds is coverage of the 73% of the person's kept pixels that have no admitted match; what it inherits is the alignment, as section 2.7 says of any map aligned to triangulated points. Orientation and an anisotropic scale at the start need a new key in §2.2 and a change to steps 8 and 9.
- **The owner's seed cannot be adopted as it is, and its substitute has a known weak point.** MotionCrafter fails the bar and predicts per camera, in each video's own frame and scale. The passing substitute, point tracks lifted with depth, takes the motion along the ray from differences of per-frame depth, and on private scene A what a per-frame depth fails to repeat from one frame to the next is of the order of the person's motion in a frame (section 2.7's control on the background; on the person the probe does not separate the two); a video depth estimator with one scale and shift per video, or a flow loss on the velocities, which needs no depth difference, are the two ways round it that the rows name.
- **For every kind of map a row needs, a passing estimator exists, except scene flow.** The papers' own choices mostly fail or are unclear (Omnidata, DSINE, DUSt3R, Marigold v1, ZoeDepth, Depth Anything V1, RAFT, MVSFormer); section 3.3's passing files stand in at no cost to the form of any loss here. Every technique also works from maps the user supplies, and each then needs what `ftgs-scene` v1 lacks: a path to a prior map per training image (§2.2).

### 3.5 Family D: dynamic-scene surfaces

*Ticket #10's section.* The survey of family D, with the cap of 60 abstracts and 12 full reads; the rows of the list of row 495 are search terms, and Split4D's paper is read, its code never.

**How the rows are read.** Columns 3 and 5 are judged against today's primitive: orientation and scale constant in time (§1.1), E1's linear motion, E3 and E4's temporal opacity with the short durations training gives it (65 % of the primitives on the person's side of private scene A last under two frames, section 2.6), and §1.3's growth and relocation. Each row says what the technique would ask of that primitive, which is one of four things: nothing, a loss over neighbours, a quantity added, or a different motion model. It also says whether the technique needs a primitive's identity to last (a canonical state, a neighbour graph fixed at the start, tracks), because E4's short durations, relocation (E10, E11) and the copied time centre and duration of D-43 all cut against that. A paper with several separable parts has its row under the part that belongs to this family, and its other parts are handed over (below). This family sits closest to the sources that are never opened. Papers that cite FreeTimeGS were read as papers. Where a paper's code implements or extends FreeTimeGS, or holds a reproduction of it, column 2 says paper only and cites a "not opened" row; of every other repository only the license file was opened. Several of the papers read run a reproduction of FreeTimeGS: FreeTimeGS++, TRiGS and Dusting the Phantom build on one, SharpTimeGS uses one for its figures, and 4DSurf runs FreeTimeGS as a baseline. What those papers publish of such runs, their behaviour and their scores, is cited in the rows as the papers' evidence, and is marked as theirs. How the reproductions were set up is not taken: that section of FreeTimeGS++ was left unread, and the appendix of TRiGS on it, read with the appendix beside it, is used for nothing (rows 766, 768, 769, 771, 773).

**Screening table.**

| Technique | 1. Weights' license | 2. Code license | 3. Integration class | 4. Render backends | 5. Fit with the primitives | 6. Cameras that disagree | 7. Prior maps | 8. Evidence | 9. Training cost |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Split4D** (Hu et al., ACM TOG 44(6), 2025; arXiv 2512.22745), the owner's search seed: a feature per primitive on a frozen FreeTimeGS-style reconstruction, trained by a contrastive loss against per-image instance masks, with the features of primitives about to fade aligned to their nearest neighbours at the next frame (rows 745, 746) | SAM, for the per-image masks: passes for each of its three files (section 3.3, SAM); the paper names no file. DINOv2, for an early alignment loss: passes (this section's estimators table, DINOv2); the paper names no file. SAM2 and Grounded-SAM-2 make the evaluation masks only (row 746) | Paper only. The code builds on FreeTimeGS and is never opened; the pages read link none (row 765) | A change to a quantity or to motion: one more tensor per primitive, a feature of 32 values, with a loss on its composited map, so also a loss that needs new render outputs (row 746). In the paper the reconstruction is trained first and frozen, and only the features train: E1 to E17 are not touched, and with the field off nothing of §1.1 or the §3 tests changes. As published it places no primitive: it yields groups of primitives per object, which a rigidity or shared-motion loss could read, and the paper proposes no such loss | The feature composited with E6's weights as 32 more channels: section 2.4, cell 8a for the gsplat backend and 8e for the reference backend; through Q-226's device three channels a pass (cell 8d), so eleven passes. No depth, normal or distortion output (row 746) | Written for this primitive: E1's motion and E4's temporal opacity are the paper's own equations, though it describes the primitive with a 4D scale and two quaternions (row 746; §1.1). Short durations are what it works around: the paper says the primitives of one object are replaced altogether over longer intervals, so a primitive's identity does not last, and it pairs each primitive about to fade at frame t with its k-nearest neighbours at t + 1 instead, a pairing over time that needs no canonical space, no track and no neighbour graph fixed at the start (row 746; E4). Relocation and split do not run while the features train; trained jointly, a relocated copy or a split child would have to take its target's or parent's feature (E11, E17, D-43), and the paper reports that joint training lowers PSNR from 32.2 to 24.3 dB on one scene (row 746) | The masks need not agree between cameras or over time, which is the paper's point: per-image masks replace video segmentation, and labels from a video tracker with visible errors still train it (row 746). It assumes a good reconstruction, from 19 to 24 cameras; no evidence with few cameras, inconsistent images or pixels known to be wrong | Instance masks for every training image, and DINOv2 feature maps for the early loss; their cost is not stated. Masks the user supplies would do: the loss reads only which instance a pixel belongs to (row 746) | Neural3DV, Multi-Human and SelfCap; mIoU against masks made with SAM2. Table 2, one scene (Juggle), mIoU: 0.895 in full; 0.866 without the neighbour alignment; 0.858 without DINOv2; 0.552 with training images drawn at random instead of in temporal order; 0.644 with 4DGS's motion model in place of the linear one. All of it measures segmentation; nothing measures geometry (row 746) | A second stage after the reconstruction: 10,000 iterations, about 30 minutes a scene on an RTX 4090. Per-iteration cost against the default training configuration (§3.4 B-1): not stated (row 746) |
| **FreeTimeGS++, the persistence gate** ("gated marginalization"; arXiv 2605.03337, 2026), a FreeTimeGS follow-up: a learnable gate g per primitive, and the temporal opacity g + (1 − g)·σt(t) in place of σt(t), so that a primitive can stay visible for the whole clip (row 766). Its two other additions are handed over (below) | None: the gate uses no estimator (row 766) | Paper only. The paper says its code is released; it reproduces and extends FreeTimeGS, so the code and the project page are never opened (row 767) | A change to a quantity or to motion: one more scalar per primitive and a change to E4's σt(t), with a regularizer on the gates whose form the sections read do not give (row 766). With the gate held at 0 the expression is E4's, so a field that defaults to off leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched. With it on, E3 and E4 change, which are [P], and E8 with them, whose weight on each primitive is σt(t) | Nothing new: the gated product reaches the render backends as E3's opacity does today. No depth, normal or distortion output (row 766) | Built on this primitive and aimed at its short durations. The paper's analysis of its authors' reproduction: after training, one group of primitives is visible for nearly the whole clip and renders the static background, and a broad group of short-lived ones renders the moving subject; and a clean render can hide a noisy velocity map, because different primitives explain the same content at different times (row 766). So short durations are how this primitive carries a moving subject, and the gate concerns the persistent group: nothing in the paper lengthens the primitives on the subject. Constant orientation and scale and E1 stay; what E1 does to a moving primitive whose gate is near 1 is not stated. A relocated copy or a split child would take its target's or parent's gate with the other tensors (D-15, D-43; this reading). On relocation itself the paper compares what a copy inherits and finds 3DGS-MCMC's recomputed opacity and scale, E11's choice, steadier than an exact copy, which leaves a haze in static regions (row 766) | Not addressed by the gate. Dense rigs only (18 to 24 cameras); no evidence with few cameras, inconsistent images or pixels known to be wrong (row 766) | None: the gate reads no prior map (row 766) | DyNeRF and SelfCap, PSNR. Table 5, one run a line: adding the gate moves PSNR from 32.73 to 32.78 dB and from 26.25 to 26.41 dB, less than the spread the paper reports between ten runs of its baseline (0.22 and 0.36 dB), with LPIPS slightly worse (0.031 to 0.034, 0.138 to 0.139). No experiment isolates the gate over repeated runs, and nothing measures geometry (row 766) | Not stated. One scalar per primitive, and no new render pass (row 766; this reading) |
| **SharpTimeGS** (arXiv 2602.02989, 2026), a FreeTimeGS follow-up: a learnable lifespan radius r per primitive; a flat-top temporal opacity, 1 within r of the time centre and a Gaussian fall-off beyond; a velocity divided by a function of the lifespan, so that long-lived primitives stay put; a loss that lengthens lifespans; and a densification scored by velocity over lifespan (row 768) | For its initialization only: RAFT, unclear, so treated as not passing (section 3.3, RAFT); SAM2, passes (section 3.3, SAM 2). The paper names no weights file. COLMAP is a program (§2.3), not an estimator (row 768) | Paper only: the pages read link no code. The authors say they reproduced FreeTimeGS's pipeline, so code of theirs would hold a reproduction and would stay unopened (row 768) | A change to a quantity or to motion: one more scalar per primitive, E4 replaced by the flat-top profile, and E1's velocity divided by f = 1 + max(1, (σ + r)²). With it, a change to growth, relocation or split: after the first third of training the count is fixed, low-opacity primitives are removed and as many are cloned from the top of a score. And one part that is only a loss on today's primitives: the mean of the reciprocal of each primitive's lifespan, which with r = 0 reads the durations alone (row 768; E1, E4, E8, E10). A field that defaults to off, and then bypasses the profile, the divisor and the new allocation, leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched; no value of r gives E1 back, since the divisor as printed is never below 2. The lifespan loss alone would be a new term of E9 with weight 0 by default | The representation needs nothing new: opacity and position are formed before the renderer, as E3 and E1 are today. Its two PGSR-style losses do: the flattening loss reads scales only, and the single-view consistency of normals and depth reads a normal map and a depth map (section 2.4, cells 4a and 5a, or 4d and 5d through Q-226's device); which depth PGSR's loss takes is section 3.1's to say (row 768) | Written against this primitive, with orientation and scale constant as in §1.1 (row 768). On short durations it argues the other side of the shell: under a Gaussian profile a long-lived surface needs several overlapping primitives, so training keeps inserting them, and its lifespan loss is there to make one primitive serve longer instead of being broken into several. Its moving points still start short, with a lifespan of three frames, against one frame interval at D-21's default stride. The division of v by f ties motion to duration, which E1 does not. Its second stage stands where relocation does: removal by low opacity and cloning from a score that adds velocity over lifespan to error and opacity, in place of E10's ρ and E11's move; its first stage clones by AbsGS's criterion, not by E10's g. A copy would take r as it takes the duration (D-43; this reading) | Not addressed. Synchronized dense rigs, 18 to 24 cameras, and an initialization that runs COLMAP per frame on the masked moving objects, which needs cameras that agree. No evidence with few cameras, inconsistent images or pixels known to be wrong (row 768) | For initialization: optical flow to find moving points and masks of the moving objects, per camera and frame; cost not stated. Masks the user supplies would do, since the flow only prompts the masks (row 768) | Neural3DV, ENeRF-Outdoor and SelfCap; PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS. Table 2, part of SelfCap, PSNR: 27.36 dB in full; 26.76 with the Gaussian profile (no r); 26.82 with 4DGS's densification; 26.83 without the velocity-aware initialization; 25.96 with 4DGS's coupled primitive. Against FreeTimeGS's published figures: + 0.4 to + 0.6 dB. No ablation isolates the lifespan loss, the division of the velocity or the PGSR-style losses, and nothing measures geometry (row 768) | Several hours a scene, which the paper gives as a limitation; per-iteration cost against the default training configuration not stated (row 768) |
| **4DSurf** (CVPR 2026; arXiv 2603.28064), a paper that cites FreeTimeGS: flat primitives in a canonical shape per short segment of the clip, moved, turned and resized by a velocity field (an MLP), with a loss that makes each primitive's motion along its normal agree with the change of the rendered depth over time ("SDF flow regularization"), and segments trained in order, each starting from the last (row 769) | None named: the masks it trains on are the datasets' own (row 769) | The repository's license file is MIT. Nothing else of it was opened: the paper builds on Dynamic-2DGS's code, whose license was not read, and runs FreeTimeGS as a baseline. Taken from the paper (rows 769, 770) | As published, a change to a quantity or to motion: 2DGS's flat primitives (two scales, §1.1, E5, E6), a canonical shape with an MLP that gives each primitive a velocity, an angular velocity and an expansion velocity in place of E1, and a new model every 5 timesteps. No field's default covers that. Its regularizer alone is a loss that needs new render outputs: it reads a primitive's velocity, a normal per primitive, the primitive's own depth and the rendered depth at its centre's pixel at two nearby times (row 769). On today's primitives, with E1's `velocities` as the motion, that loss would be a new term of E9, off by default; nobody has run it so, and it needs a normal, which a primitive of three free scales does not have (section 3.1's matter) | Depth: 2DGS's rendered depth, the weighted mean of the depths where the ray meets each disk, at two times per step. For today's primitives the nearest outputs are expected depth (section 2.4, cells 2a and 2d), read at the pixels of the primitives' centres, as Q-230 samples the ground truth's depth. Its other regularizers are 2DGS's normal alignment and depth distortion: cells 4a, 5a and, for the pairwise distortion, 6c on flat primitives only, an open question with the owner for today's (cells 6a, 6d; cell 7a for the squared form). `rasterization_2dgs` supplies all of it for flat primitives (column c) (row 769) | Poor as published, close in its loss. Orientation and scale change with time here: the field turns each primitive and resizes it, which §1.1 excludes. A primitive lives through its whole segment and is handed to the next, so identity lasts where E4's short durations end it; the loss itself asks only that a primitive have a velocity and a normal at one time and that depth can be rendered just before and after, which a short-lived primitive of E1 satisfies while it is visible (this reading; E1, E4). Relocation and split are not discussed; the paper adds primitives where content appears between segments (row 769) | It trains from few cameras, on people: 8 on Hi4D and 10 on CMU Panoptic, all used for training. It assumes they agree: foreground masks of every view build a visual hull to start from, and a mask loss holds the alpha. On the same data it reports FreeTimeGS's meshes at 1.78 to 3.21 cm of Chamfer distance per scene against its own 0.47 to 0.84 cm, with surfaces that jitter and drift over time; how that baseline was run is in the supplementary material, not read. No evidence with inconsistent images or pixels known to be wrong (row 769) | Foreground masks for every training image, for the visual hull and the mask loss; the datasets supply them, and masks the user supplies would do (row 769) | Hi4D and CMU Panoptic, Chamfer distance of meshes against scans. Table 3, Hi4D, overall: 1.49 cm with the velocity field and 2DGS's regularizers; 1.02 with the SDF-flow regularization added; 0.77 with independent segments; 0.67 with overlapping segments. So the loss is isolated, on flat primitives with a canonical shape, and is worth a third of the error there. Spread of the error over time: 0.18 cm against 1.19 for Dynamic-2DGS (row 769) | 30,000 iterations, about 30 minutes on an RTX 3090 Ti, for each segment of 5 timesteps: some 12 hours for 118 timesteps (this arithmetic). The loss's own cost is not stated; on today's primitives it would add depth passes at two times (section 2.4, "The cost") (row 769) |
| **TRiGS** (arXiv 2604.00538, 2026), a FreeTimeGS follow-up: in place of E1, each primitive carries its own rigid motion over its visible window, a rotation about a learnable local anchor with a translation along the axis, from a base coefficient and a quadratic Bézier residual, so that its covariance turns with time; a loss aligns the base motions of spatial neighbours; relocation draws its sources by a score with motion cues (row 771) | None new: the initial points come from RoMa, the package's own dense matcher (D-58, §2.3). No prior-map estimator (row 771) | Paper only. The paper says its codebase holds the authors' reproduction of FreeTimeGS, which TRiGS extends; the code and the project page are never opened (row 772) | A change to a quantity or to motion: E1 replaced, and §1.1's constant orientation with it, since the covariance becomes R(t)·Σ·R(t)ᵀ; 27 values per primitive (an anchor, a base coefficient and three control coefficients of se(3)) stand where `velocities` has 3 (row 771; this count). With a field off the motion stays E1 and nothing of E1 to E17 or the §3 tests moves; on, it changes two [P] items, and the per-time arrays gain a rotation per time. Two parts could stand alone. Its rigidity loss is only a loss on today's primitives once the angular term is dropped: over each primitive's 3 nearest neighbours by centre, the squared difference of velocities, weighted by the likeness of their DC colours; it reads `means`, `velocities` and `sh0`, and would be a term of E9 with weight 0 by default; the paper does not run it on linear motion. Its relocation is a change to growth, relocation or split: four cues in place of E10's two, and clones that copy everything with scales cut to about 66% in place of E11 | Nothing new: the rotated covariance reaches the render backends as a quaternion per primitive and time. No depth, normal or distortion output (row 771) | Built on this primitive, and against two of its properties. Constant orientation: replaced by a rotation over time; scale stays constant. E1: the paper says translation-only linear motion makes a curved trajectory need many primitives in pieces, so that identity over time is lost, and replaces it. Short durations: the residual lives on a window of two temporal scales either side of the time centre, and the aim is fewer, longer-lived primitives (0.5 M for 1,200 frames, where its linear-motion baseline grows to 2.1 M). The rigidity loss takes neighbours by 3D centre; whether time enters the search is not stated, and among short-lived primitives a neighbour in space can live at another time (this reading; E4). Relocation is kept, every 100 iterations at an opacity threshold of 0.005 by the paper's own settings, with its own score and copy rule; growth is dropped for a fixed count, where D-18 grows (row 771) | Not addressed. Calibrated dense rigs, 19 to 24 cameras; no evidence with few cameras, inconsistent images or pixels known to be wrong (row 771) | None: it reads no prior map; its initial points come from the dense matcher, as today's do (row 771) | SelfCap lengthened to 600 to 1,200 frames, and Neural3DV; PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS. Table 4, 1,200 frames: linear motion 24.83 dB with 2.10 M primitives; rotation and translation as independent parameters about the origin 23.95; local se(3) 25.52 with 0.60 M; full 26.05 with 0.50 M; the paper says its ablated baselines, the linear and the independent-parameter arms among them, were allowed to densify while the full model was held at 0.5 M, so the comparison is not at equal count. Table 5, from the full model: without the rigidity loss 25.48 dB, with what the text calls surface tearing; without the smoothness loss 25.66; without the relocation score 25.80. Nothing measures geometry (row 771) | 30,000 iterations on an RTX 4090; the time is not stated, and the paper gives the length of per-scene optimization as its limitation. The neighbour search's cost is not stated (row 771) |
| **Dusting the Phantom** (arXiv 2610.21302, 2026), a FreeTimeGS follow-up: two changes to where and when capacity goes, at a fixed count. A relocated copy is placed at a time drawn along its target's trajectory, by the target's gradient at each timestamp ("Temporal Biased Relocation"); and, once in training, low-opacity primitives are re-initialized at points triangulated from badly reconstructed moving pixels of several cameras ("Seed Injection") (row 773) | None for training: the mask of moving pixels is a difference of adjacent training frames. SAM3 makes the masks of an evaluation metric only, and fails the bar (section 3.3, SAM 3) (row 773) | Paper only. The paper builds on a reproduced FreeTimeGS implementation; its code and project page are never opened (row 774) | A change to growth, relocation or split, and nothing else: the paper keeps the representation. The temporal rule keeps E10's choice of the dead primitive and of its target and changes what the copy inherits: a time centre drawn among the target's timestamps, the position E1 gives the target at that time, and a duration no longer than the target's, where D-15's copies take the target's time centre and duration. It needs the 2D-mean gradient kept per primitive and per timestamp, where E10 keeps one sum per primitive. Seed Injection is a new event with relocation suspended around it (row 773; E10, E11, D-15). A field that defaults to off leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched | Nothing new: it reads the per-pixel L1 error of the render and the gradient of the 2D means, which `render()` already returns for E10's statistics (§2.1, `RenderOutput`). No depth, normal or distortion output (row 773) | Made for this primitive: orientation, scale, E1, E3 and E4 are untouched. On short durations it pushes the shell's way: a relocated copy's duration is its target's or shorter, and an injected primitive starts faint and short (opacity 0.05, duration 0.03), by design, to catch small fast objects. Its diagnosis of relocation is the useful part here: E10 and E11 decide where a copy goes and never when, so capacity lands at the target's own time (row 773; this reading, with section 2.6 for the shell's measures). Split (E17) is not touched | The temporal rule assumes nothing about the cameras. Seed Injection assumes they agree: it pairs rays of different cameras by their closest approach and keeps the consistent pairs, so cameras whose images disagree would yield fewer seeds or seeds at a compromise position (this reading). It was run with 8 of 23 to 27 cameras; on one frame shown it fails with 2 and works with 4. No evidence with inconsistent images or pixels known to be wrong (row 773) | None: it reads no prior map; its mask of moving pixels is a difference of training frames (row 773) | N3DV, CMU Panoptic (27 training cameras) and two captures of fast small objects; PSNR, a PSNR on masked moving regions, LPIPS. Table 4, mean of three CMU sequences, one run each: baseline 28.22 dB and LPIPS 0.161; with Seed Injection 28.53 and 0.160; with the temporal rule 28.57 and 0.080; with both 28.98 and 0.082. The temporal rule also needs fewer relocations. Nothing measures geometry, and the gains are on small fast objects, not on a body's surface (row 773) | Measured: 5.69 minutes for the baseline against 5.74 with the temporal rule, 5.85 with Seed Injection and 5.91 with both, at 500 K primitives, 50,000 iterations and 640 × 360. The memory of a gradient per primitive and timestamp is not stated (row 773) |
| **Relaxed Rigidity with Ray-based Grouping** (Junoh et al., arXiv 2603.24994, 2026): two losses over the Gaussians that one pixel's ray meets with a blending weight above a threshold. One asks each to move in the direction of its group's mean displacement, magnitudes left free; the other asks the eigenvalues of the covariance of the group's positions to stay the same between two times. No flow, depth or track is used (row 775) | None: no estimator (row 775) | Paper only: the paper links no code (row 775) | As published, new rasterization kernels: the groups, their mean displacement and their covariance are formed inside the rasterizer, by a module added to the 3DGS one, and gsplat 1.5.3's compositing kernels keep no set of primitives per pixel (section 2.4, cells 3a and 6a describe the same lack). Whether decision 1's third edge then bars it from the finalists turns on the pair-list question, which is open with the owner (column 4). What it asks of the primitive is nothing: positions at two times, which E1 gives for any time. With E1 the displacement is v·Δt, so the first loss is the cosine between a primitive's velocity and its group's mean velocity (this reading; E1). A variant that composites displacement and position moments with E6's weights, instead of thresholding them, would be a loss that needs new render outputs (section 2.4, cell 8a); it is not the paper's loss and nobody has run it | Per pixel, the set of primitives with weight above a threshold, and sums over that set. Not supplied by D-75's stage functions, by `rasterization()` or by Q-226's device, which composite and do not select; the only route in gsplat 1.5.3 is the pair list, unmeasured and open with the owner (section 2.4, "The pair list"). The reference backend holds every pixel's weights and could form the groups in PyTorch (section 2.4, column e) (row 775) | It needs no identity that lasts: the groups are rebuilt at every render from the weights, with no canonical space, neighbour graph or track, which suits primitives that relocate and split (E11, E17). It was run on models with a canonical space or spline trajectories, where every primitive exists at both times. With E4's short durations a group's members at time t may have faded at the second time; E1 still gives their positions there, and whether the losses mean anything then is untested. The second time is drawn within m timesteps of the first, with m set between 2 and 20 per dataset, a window wider than the two frames a short-lived primitive lasts. Constant orientation and scale are not touched (row 775; this reading, E4) | It needs no agreement between cameras: a group comes from one camera's ray, and the losses compare the group with itself. Tested with one or two cameras only (D-NeRF's moving camera, HyperNeRF's and NeRF-DS's two-camera rigs), never on a multi-camera rig; no evidence with inconsistent images or pixels known to be wrong (row 775) | None: it reads no prior map, which is the paper's point (row 775) | D-NeRF, HyperNeRF, NeRF-DS; PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS. Table 3 isolates each part on one baseline (PSNR on D-NeRF and on HyperNeRF): 35.36 and 25.17 without; 36.46 and 25.30 with both losses on ray groups; 36.23 and 24.23 with the direction loss alone; 34.98 and 24.91 with the eigenvalue loss alone. The same table is the family's evidence against rigidity over nearest neighbours: an as-rigid-as-possible loss on nearest-neighbour groups lowers PSNR to 34.80 and 23.18, and the paper's two losses on nearest-neighbour groups to 33.29 and 25.11. Over four baselines the gain is 0.06 to 2.35 dB. Nothing measures geometry (row 775) | 2.3 times the baseline's training time on D-NeRF and 3.6 times on HyperNeRF with both losses (841 to 1,924 s; 1,963 to 7,138 s); 2.0 and 2.3 times with the direction loss alone. Most of it is the covariance and its SVD in rasterization (row 775) |
| **4D-Rotor Gaussian Splatting, and its 4D consistency loss** (Duan et al., SIGGRAPH 2024; arXiv 2402.03307), a search seed for orientation that changes with time: 4D Gaussians rotated by a rotor and sliced at each time into 3D Gaussians, trained with an entropy loss on opacity and a loss that pulls each primitive's velocity toward the mean velocity of its 8 nearest neighbours in space and time (row 776) | None: no estimator; the initial points are COLMAP's or random (row 776) | The repository's license file is Apache-2.0, so its source is readable under the rules; nothing but the license file was opened, and whether it bundles code under other licenses was not checked (row 777) | The 4D consistency loss is a loss on today's primitives: it reads a velocity per primitive and a position in space and time for the neighbour search, which are `velocities`, `means` and `times`. As a term of E9 with weight 0 by default it leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched. The representation is a change to a quantity in form only, and the seed's premise fails: the slice of a 4D Gaussian at time t has a covariance that does not depend on t, a centre that moves linearly and a Gaussian decay in t, which is §1.1's primitive with E1 and E4 under another parameterization, its velocity tied to the 4D covariance instead of stored. A 4D rotation does not make orientation change with time (row 776; §1.1, E1, E4, E9) | Nothing: the slice is formed before the renderer and the loss reads stored tensors. The neighbour search runs outside the render backends (row 776) | The same primitive once sliced, so the loss was designed for primitives that exist around one time: neighbours are searched in space and time together, each scaled by the scene's extent, because, the paper says, neighbours in space alone can belong to objects that move differently. It needs no identity that lasts, no canonical space and no track. With relocation and split the neighbours change (E11, E17); how often the paper recomputes them is not stated. Its premise on motion is E1's: linear over a short interval, several primitives for anything more (row 776) | Not addressed for the loss. Tested on dense rigs (Plenoptic Video) and on one moving camera (D-NeRF). The paper recommends its entropy loss for opaque surfaces and sparse views, and says training on batches of 2 or 3 views helps geometric consistency with sparse views, where D-26 renders one image an iteration. No evidence with inconsistent images or pixels known to be wrong (row 776) | None: neither the loss nor the representation reads a prior map (row 776) | D-NeRF (synthetic, one moving camera) and Plenoptic Video; PSNR. Table 3, D-NeRF: 31.50 dB with the entropy loss; 31.91 with the 4D consistency loss added; 33.06 with batch training added. So the loss is isolated at + 0.41 dB, on monocular synthetic data and with the entropy loss on; on multi-camera data it is not isolated, and the figures show smoother flow, not better surfaces. The conclusion says floaters and inconsistent motions remain. Nothing measures geometry (row 776) | Not stated for the loss: a search for 8 neighbours in 4D over all primitives, at an interval the paper does not give. The whole method trains Plenoptic scenes in 60 minutes in its own CUDA framework (row 776) |
| **Dynamic 3D Gaussians** (Luiten et al., arXiv 2308.09713, 2023), a search seed for local rigidity: a centre and a rotation per primitive and timestep, with size, colour and opacity fixed for the whole clip, trained one timestep after another under three losses over each primitive's 20 nearest neighbours: local rigidity, rotation similarity and isometry (row 778) | None: no estimator. Its foreground masks come from differencing each frame with an image of the empty scene, and its initial points from depth cameras (row 778) | Paper only: no license was read. The paper's project page was not opened and a request at a guessed repository address found no license file; the paper renders with 3DGS's renderer, and the PRD expects code built on the original 3DGS to be documentation only (row 778) | A change to a quantity or to motion, and of the whole method: positions and rotations stored per timestep in place of E1; no time centre or duration, so every primitive lasts the clip, in place of E3 and E4; training timestep by timestep with the count fixed after the first, in place of §1.3's growth and relocation. No field's default covers that. Its losses ask two things today's primitive lacks: a rotation per time, and neighbours that stay the same primitives over the clip. Between two primitives alive at one time, with §1.1's constant orientation and E1, the local-rigidity term reduces to the difference of their velocities times the time step: the demand of the 4D consistency loss in 4D-Rotor Gaussian Splatting's row, which is a loss on today's primitives (row 778; this reading, §1.1, E1) | Nothing new for the three losses: they read stored tensors. Its mask loss composites a logit per primitive (section 2.4, cell 8a), and it reads depth by rendering each primitive's centre depth as its colour, Q-226's device, for tracking only (row 778) | Poor. The method rests on identity that lasts: a one-to-one correspondence of every primitive across all timesteps, a neighbour graph and weights fixed at the first timestep, and no densification, relocation or split afterwards (E10, E11, E17 have no place). E4's short durations and relocation both break the graph. Orientation changes with time here, which §1.1 excludes; scale is constant, as in §1.1. The paper's own limit is the same point from the other side: whatever is not visible in the first frame cannot be reconstructed (row 778) | It assumes many cameras that agree: 27 training cameras, and the paper says it does not work on monocular video. It does absorb one disagreement, colour: a scale and an offset per channel and camera, fitted at the first timestep, which is family E's matter. No evidence with few cameras, inconsistent images or pixels known to be wrong (row 778) | None from an estimator: a foreground mask per image, here by frame differencing against an image of the empty scene; masks the user supplies would do (row 778) | Six sequences of the Panoptic Studio; PSNR on 4 test cameras and the error of 3D tracks. Table 3, one sequence: 29.48 dB and 1.90 cm in full; 28.51 dB and 4.32 cm without local rigidity; 29.43 and 1.91 without rotation similarity; 29.36 and 1.93 without isometry; 24.14 and 8.46 without the mask loss. So local rigidity is isolated, at about 1 dB and half the tracking error, and the two other losses at next to nothing. Nothing measures the surface (row 778) | Two hours for 150 timesteps of 200,000 to 300,000 primitives at 640 × 360: 10,000 iterations for the first timestep and 2,000 for each later one (row 778) |
| **Vidu4D, Dynamic Gaussian Surfels** (Wang et al., NeurIPS 2024; arXiv 2405.16822), a search seed: from one generated video of an object, flat surfels in a static state, warped to each time by 25 bones whose rigid transforms an MLP predicts, with a normal consistency loss applied in the warped state and learned refinements of rotation and scale; the camera poses and the warping come from a first stage with a neural signed-distance field (row 779) | DINOv2 features, to register the cameras in the first stage: passes (this section's estimators table, DINOv2); the paper names no file. The video generator is the input's source, outside the reconstruction (row 779) | The repository's license file is Apache-2.0, so its source is readable under the rules; nothing but the license file was opened, and whether it bundles code under other licenses was not checked (row 780) | A change to a quantity or to motion, and of the whole method: flat primitives (§1.1, E5, E6), a static state every primitive belongs to for the whole clip, and a warping field that moves and turns each primitive in place of E1, E3 and E4. No field's default covers that. Its one surface term is the normal consistency of 2DGS and Gaussian surfels, a loss that needs new render outputs, taken at the rendered time after the warp; on today's primitives that is section 3.1's loss rendered at the training image's time, with nothing left that is specific to motion (row 779; this reading) | A normal per primitive composited, and normals from finite differences of the depth map, at each rendered time: section 2.4, cells 4a and 5a, or 4d and 5d through Q-226's device; column c for flat primitives (row 779) | Poor. Identity lasts the clip, through the static state; orientation changes with time, since the warp turns each surfel's frame and normal with the surface, which §1.1 excludes; there is no temporal opacity, and growth, relocation and split are not discussed. What it shows about §1.1: a primitive of constant orientation cannot follow a surface that turns, so with E1 and E4 a turning surface is covered by a succession of short-lived primitives, each with its own orientation (row 779; this reading, §1.1, E4) | Built for images that are not consistent views of one rigid scene: one video from a video generator, where the paper says the background deforms and flickers and COLMAP rarely registers the frames. It treats the inconsistency as deformation of the object over time and learns the cameras. That is one camera per time: there are never two cameras at one time to disagree, which is private scene A's case (section 2.5). Evidence on 30 generated videos; none with several cameras or pixels known to be wrong (row 779) | None as maps: DINOv2 features for the registration; their cost is not stated (row 779) | 30 generated videos; PSNR on frames held out between training frames: 27.30 ± 2.66 dB against 24.75 for SC-GS and 22.63 for Deformable-GS given the same poses. Table 3, three videos: the refinement of rotation and scale is worth 0.4 to 1.6 dB, the first stage 3 to 5 dB. The warped-state normal loss is ablated in a figure only, and nothing measures geometry (row 779) | Over an hour a video on an A800, for both stages (row 779) |
| **MonoFusion** (Wang et al., ICCV 2025; arXiv 2507.23782), a search seed for training from few cameras: four static cameras 90° apart, on people; canonical Gaussians moved and turned by 28 shared SE(3) motion bases; monocular depth per camera and frame fitted by a scale and a shift to multi-view point maps on the background; motion bases started from clusters of image features; losses on the rendered depth, mask and features, and a rigidity loss over nearest neighbours (row 781) | DUSt3R, the metric target of the alignment: fails (section 3.3, DUSt3R). MoGe, the monocular depth: passes (section 3.3, MoGe). DINOv2, small with registers, for the features: passes (this section's estimators table, DINOv2). The masks of moving objects come from a user-prompted model the sections read do not name. The paper names no weights file (row 781) | Paper only: no license file was found at the repository's root, so nothing of it was opened (row 782) | As a whole, a change to a quantity or to motion: a canonical frame, shared motion bases in place of E1, and colour and opacity fixed for the clip in place of E3 and E4; no field's default covers that. Two parts stand apart. Its depth, aligned between cameras and over time, used to start the primitives and as a loss on the rendered depth, is a technique on prior maps: section 3.4's, named here. Its rigidity loss keeps the distances between a primitive and its nearest neighbours equal at two times; between two of today's primitives alive at both times that is a condition on their relative velocity, as in the row of Dynamic 3D Gaussians (row 781; this reading, E1) | A rendered depth, of a kind the paper does not state: for today's primitives, accumulated or expected depth (section 2.4, cells 1a and 2a, or 1d and 2d). A rendered mask and a 32-channel feature map, composited like colours (cell 8a; three channels a pass through Q-226's device, cell 8d) (row 781) | Poor for its motion model: identity lasts the clip through the canonical frame; position and orientation change with time, through the bases, while scale, opacity and colour stay; no temporal opacity, growth or relocation, and only 18,000 primitives on the people. What it says about few cameras bears on the shell: photometric supervision alone is not enough there, and depths estimated per camera conflict when unprojected and duplicate parts of the object (row 781; section 2.1 for the shell) | It is the family's evidence for few cameras on people: 4 cameras, test cameras 45° from every training camera. It assumes real, synchronized cameras with known parameters, and aligns each camera's depth to the others only by a scale and a shift fitted on the background. It reports what that cannot repair: the estimated depth of a person is often wrong seen from another camera, and no alignment of the estimators' outputs can repair that. No evidence with images that disagree about the subject, or with pixels known to be wrong (row 781) | Per camera and frame: monocular depth, multi-view point maps for each timestep, feature maps and masks of moving objects, the masks from a user prompt; costs not stated. Depth maps and masks the user supplies would do, if already aligned between cameras; DUSt3R's part has no passing substitute named in the paper (row 781; section 3.3) | Panoptic Studio (4 of its cameras) and six scenes of Ego-Exo4D with 4 cameras; PSNR, mask IoU and the error of rendered depth. Table 3: 26.19 dB without the aligned depth, 29.55 with it; 30.40 with the feature loss and the feature-clustered motion bases, the mask IoU going from 0.60 to 0.81. Table 2, cameras 45° away: 25.73 dB and a depth error of 0.188, against 23.31 and 0.273 for Dynamic 3D Gaussians started from 27 cameras' points. The rigidity loss is not ablated (row 781) | About 30 minutes for a 10 s sequence at 512 × 288 on an A6000; the estimators' cost is not stated (row 781) |

The code repository's README, section "License": the pre-trained checkpoints inherit the licenses of their training datasets and may not be used for commercial purposes, CC BY-NC-SA 4.0, https://github.com/UniFlowMatch/UFM (commit ff78d6c). **The estimators the rows name.** SAM, SAM 2, SAM 3, RAFT, DUSt3R and MoGe have their verdicts in section 3.3. UFM and DINOv2 are screened in the table above: UFM fails on its publisher's README, and DINOv2's backbone files pass. RoMa is the package's own dense matcher (§2.3, D-58). MonoFusion's masks come from a user prompt, and the sections this section read do not name the model (row 781); section 3.4's read of the paper's implementation details names SAM 2, which passes (row 728; section 3.3).

**The search seeds.** Each has a row, or the reason it has none.

- **Split4D** (the owner's): the first row. Its paper has an open version by its authors on arXiv, which was read; its code was not looked for (rows 745, 746, 765).
- **The survey arXiv 2609.39960 and its list** (the owner's): no row, because neither describes a technique of its own. The survey was read in part, as a route: it has no section on dynamic surfaces, its Table III and the list's table name the methods of round 1, and two of the list's 60 rows tick normals, DeSiRe-GS and SpectroMotion. No link of the list was followed. The survey counts as one of the 12 full reads (rows 744, 747, 748).
- **Dynamic 3D Gaussians, 4D-Rotor GS, Vidu4D, MonoFusion:** a row each; each passed the abstract screen first (rows 749, 751, 752, 753). 4D-Rotor GS was a seed for orientation that changes with time, and its row says why it is not one.
- **SC-GS:** no row. It passed the abstract screen (row 750) and was not read in full: its abstract places it as a different motion model, control points in a canonical space moved by an MLP, which is the ask of the rows of Dynamic 3D Gaussians, Vidu4D and MonoFusion, and the cap's reads went to asks those rows did not cover. Its as-rigid-as-possible loss appears in this section through another paper's ablation (the row of Relaxed Rigidity), and SC-GS is a baseline in 4DSurf's and Vidu4D's tables (rows 769, 779).
- **4C4D** (family B's seed, row 493): named here for its training from four cameras; its row is section 3.2's.

**The recency round** is rounds 2 and 9 of the log: the papers that cite FreeTimeGS, of which the 69 dated 2025-10 or later hold every one that passed the title screen, and an arXiv search restricted to submissions from 2025-10-01 (rows 755, 756, 762). It gave six of the eleven rows. Five rows are of papers that build on FreeTimeGS's primitive: Split4D, FreeTimeGS++, SharpTimeGS, TRiGS and Dusting the Phantom.

**Passed the abstract screen and not read.** 36 techniques passed the abstract screen and the cap allowed 11 papers. The 25 others have no row; what is said of them here is their abstracts' (rows 750, 754, 757 to 764), and each is a search term for whoever moves the cap.

- **A quantity added to a primitive like today's:** Spacetime Gaussians (temporal opacity with polynomial motion and rotation, the nearest published form of an angular velocity per primitive); VeGaS (a time-varying velocity kept out of the covariance, and a network that refines shapes and orientations); Ex4DGS (positions and rotations stored at keyframes and interpolated).
- **Primitives of several kinds or lifetimes:** RiGS (static, rigid and transient); Multi4D (static, persistent dynamic and transient, competing for the error).
- **Constraints on motion:** PhyMo (continuity of displacement, velocity and acceleration; its abstract says rendering supervision lets colour or opacity stand in for wrong motion); EvoGS (one learned velocity field for all primitives).
- **Shared motion in a canonical space:** SC-GS; Shape of Motion and MoSca (from one video, with depth and tracks); the SE(3) B-spline motion bases of arXiv 2603.25058; Splatography (foreground and background fields, few cameras).
- **Dynamic surfels:** Dynamic 2D Gaussians, Space-time 2D Gaussian Splatting and DynaSurfGS (flat or planar primitives deformed from a canonical state, with normal or as-rigid-as-possible terms); AT-GS (surfels trained frame by frame, with a consistency of curvature maps between consecutive frames, a loss between two renders that needs no lasting identity); DeSiRe-GS (street scenes); DySurface (a signed-distance field guided by the deformed Gaussians).
- **Meshes:** TagSplat, DG-Mesh, GauSTAR and MaGS, each binding Gaussians to a mesh or a connectivity.
- **Few cameras:** Sparse Dynamic Cameras; StudioRecon; SplatFields.

**Handed to other families.** Each is named where it was met and left to its family's section.

- **To family A (section 3.1):** the flattening loss and the single-view consistency of normals and depth that SharpTimeGS takes from PGSR (row 768); the normal alignment and depth distortion that 4DSurf takes from 2DGS, and Vidu4D's normal consistency (rows 769, 779).
- **To family B (section 3.2):** 4C4D (row 493); the entropy loss on opacity of 4D-Rotor GS, which its paper recommends for opaque surfaces and sparse views and turns off on Plenoptic Video (row 776); the pruning of short-lived primitives of 4DGS-1K (row 763).
- **To family C (section 3.4):** FreeTimeGS++'s initial velocity from optical flow, in place of E15's nearest neighbour, whose estimator, UFM, fails the bar (row 766); MonoFusion's depth aligned between cameras and over time, as initialization and as a loss on the rendered depth (row 781); UniFusion, D^2-4DGS, GC-4DGS and the challenge entry arXiv 2610.04606, which align or check estimated depth for few cameras (rows 757, 760); the depth-ordering loss of Sparse Dynamic Cameras (row 758); SharpTimeGS's initialization from flow, masks and COLMAP (row 768).
- **To family E (section 3.6):** FreeTimeGS++'s affine colour transform per camera, applied to the render in the training losses only, which over ten runs from one initialization moves PSNR from 32.47 ± 0.22 to 32.81 ± 0.08 dB and from 26.43 ± 0.36 to 27.32 ± 0.09 dB (row 766); the scale and offset per colour channel and camera of Dynamic 3D Gaussians (row 778); Phase-DGS, for cameras that are not synchronized (row 755); Flex4DHuman, which makes synchronized multi-view videos from one video with a video diffusion model and reconstructs them with an off-the-shelf 4D Gaussian splatting stage, private scene A's kind of input (row 758); the views restored or synthesized by diffusion models in the challenge entry arXiv 2610.04606 and in StudioRecon (rows 760, 758).
- **Body templates,** recorded and not pursued (decision 1): StudioRecon's people, initialized by fitting keypoints (row 758).

**How the family reached its stop rule.** By the cap of 12 full reads, with 50 of the 60 abstracts screened in 12 rounds. Two consecutive rounds that add nothing did not occur: rounds 5, 8 and 11 added none, and each was followed by a round that did. Searching stopped after round 12 because the reads were spent, so the family is cut off by its cap, not exhausted.

**What the rows say, for question 3.** These are findings from the eleven rows, not a choice of finalists, which is section 4's.

- **Coherence over time comes from identity that lasts, in most methods.** A static or canonical state (Vidu4D, MonoFusion, 4DSurf within a segment), or the same primitives stored at every timestep with a neighbour graph fixed at the start (Dynamic 3D Gaussians), with each primitive turned as the surface turns. Today's primitive has neither. The papers that build on it describe the consequence in their own words: the primitives of one object are replaced altogether over longer intervals (Split4D); short-lived primitives carry the moving subject, and the same content is explained by different primitives at different times (FreeTimeGS++); a curved trajectory is cut into many primitives (TRiGS); one long-lived surface takes several overlapping primitives (SharpTimeGS).
- **A 4D rotation does not turn a primitive over time.** The slice of a 4D Gaussian has a constant covariance and a linear motion (4D-Rotor GS). Orientation that changes with time is added per primitive, as TRiGS does with 27 values in place of a velocity, or comes from a field over a canonical state (4DSurf, Vidu4D, MonoFusion). No row reports what turning buys in geometry: TRiGS's evidence is PSNR on long clips at unequal primitive counts.
- **Three asks leave the primitive as it is.** Agreement of velocity between neighbours: the 4D consistency loss of 4D-Rotor GS, isolated at + 0.41 dB on monocular synthetic data, and TRiGS's rigidity loss, worth 0.57 dB inside TRiGS; under constant orientation and E1 the local-rigidity loss of Dynamic 3D Gaussians reduces to the same condition. Against it stands the one controlled comparison of groupings: rigidity over nearest neighbours lowered PSNR by 0.6 to 2 dB on Relaxed Rigidity's baseline, where groups taken along a ray raised it. A loss that lengthens durations: SharpTimeGS's, which no ablation isolates. And, with new render outputs, 4DSurf's agreement between a primitive's motion along its normal and the change of rendered depth, the only term here isolated on a geometry metric (1.49 to 1.02 cm), on flat primitives with a canonical shape.
- **Three added quantities could sit behind a field that defaults to off:** FreeTimeGS++'s gate, SharpTimeGS's lifespan radius and TRiGS's rotation. Only the gate gives today's equation back at a value of its own, E4 at a gate of 0. For the two others the field would have to bypass the new form: SharpTimeGS's divisor of the velocity is never 1 as printed, and the sections read of TRiGS do not show its motion reducing to E1. Each changes [P] items when on. The gate's measured gain is inside its paper's run-to-run spread.
- **Relocation is changed by three of the five follow-ups:** when a copy is placed (Dusting the Phantom), by what score and with what copy (TRiGS, SharpTimeGS). FreeTimeGS++ compares what a copy inherits and finds E11's choice the steadier.
- **Few cameras, on people, have two rows,** 4DSurf (8 and 10 cameras) and MonoFusion (4), and both assume cameras that agree. 4DSurf reports FreeTimeGS's surfaces, as it ran it, at 1.3 to 1.5 times its own Chamfer distance on CMU Panoptic and 3 to 5 times on Hi4D; MonoFusion reports that the estimated depth of a person is often wrong seen from another camera. No row has evidence with several cameras whose images disagree at one time; Vidu4D's generated video has one camera per time.
- **Geometry is seldom measured.** 4DSurf (Chamfer distance), MonoFusion (the error of rendered depth) and Dynamic 3D Gaussians (3D tracks) measure something besides image metrics. None of the five papers that build on FreeTimeGS's primitive does.

### 3.6 Family E: cameras whose images disagree

*Ticket #11's section.* The survey of the adjacent family E, with the cap of 120 abstracts and 24 full reads; the setting is decision 4's: no camera of private scene A is a real video or trusted above the others, and some of its pixels are placeholder pixels.

**The setting, in the parts the rows answer to.** Section 2.5 measured it, and columns 5 and 6 name its parts in these words:

- **The background's part.** The manifest's cameras are not quite the cameras the images show. A turn of one camera of each pair by 0.1° to 0.4°, fitted on the background's matches in the even frames, brings the background's median residual from 0.29 to 2.24 px down to 0.3 to 0.6 px on the odd ones. A correction per camera could absorb it.
- **The person's part.** After that turn the person's matches still lie 0.9 to 4.0 px from epipolar agreement at the median, 2 to 13 times the background's, unevenly over the frames, on the fifth of the crop the matcher can match. Frame offsets explain none of it. What could act on it is a deformation per camera or per time, or a lower weight per pixel or region, which needs a signal for where the images disagree.
- **Placeholder pixels.** One camera only, `cam0`: 0.263% of its pixels, all inside the person's crop, with a mask per frame already computed. They can be left out of E7.
- **Colour.** Decision 4 says the nine cameras share their colour temperature and sharpness, and section 2.5 measured no colour difference between cameras. A colour correction per camera answers a part nobody has measured.

**Three readings that hold for every row,** cited in the cells as readings 1 to 3. They are this ticket's readings of the spec and of the code (section 2.3), not findings of a paper.

1. **The integration classes.** The five classes of column 3 name no trainable parameters per camera (a pose, a colour transform, an embedding, a deformation) and no second model trained beside the first. A row of that kind gives the nearest class and says what the class leaves out; ticket #13 may want a sixth class for them.
2. **Weights and masks.** `total_loss` takes no mask and no weight per pixel, and the manifest reader refuses a `mask` on a training image (section 2.3; §2.2). A row that weights or masks pixels therefore changes §2.1's `total_loss` and, where the masks come as files, §2.2; with its field off nothing changes. Four things follow whichever technique supplies the weight:
   - The mask has to be defined for each of E7's three terms: L1, per pixel; SSIM, whose 11 × 11 valid window mixes kept and masked pixels (D-8); and the perceptual network, which reads whole images (D-89). Q-233 met the same question for the metrics and reports the kept-pixel PSNR alone, with SSIM and ꟻLIP not masked.
   - E10's accumulated gradient g, which chooses growth's candidates and relocation's targets, comes from the same loss, so pixels with a lower weight ask for fewer primitives.
   - E8 is not a per-pixel term. A primitive whose pixels are masked in every image loses the render's gradient and keeps E8's pull on its opacity, so it fades below D-14's threshold and is relocated (E10, E11).
   - A technique whose signal marks the person, in every camera and frame, as the content that disagrees would therefore remove the person. Each such row says so in column 6: it is a finding, not a fit.
3. **Test views.** E16 and D-67 score a test image against the render at the manifest's camera, over the whole image or D-67's crop. A correction fitted per training camera (a pose, a colour transform, a deformation) does not exist for a held-out camera. Scored without it, the model is charged for the held-out camera's own offset; scored with a correction fitted on the test image, the score depends on a fit to the image being scored. No camera is ground truth (decision 4), so a row with such a correction says in column 8 what its test-view score would mean.

A row is one technique. A paper with two techniques of different integration classes has two rows, and counts as one full read.

Its two companions are of other classes: utilization-based pruning is a change to growth, relocation or split, handed to section 3.2, and its affine colour transform per image is a correction per camera (WildGaussians' second row). Its replacement for 3DGS's opacity reset has no object here: opacities are never reset (§1.3 Growth), and relocation controls the count | It assumes a static scene whose images agree but for transient objects that come and go, and that inliers are the majority: the mask starts from the residuals above a quantile (the median for the upper label, the 0.9 quantile for the lower). The signal needs no trusted camera; it needs the outliers to be a minority and the subject not to be among them. On private scene A the residual is highest where the cameras disagree, on the person, in every camera and frame: the classifier would learn the person's features as the distractor and leave the person out of the loss, and reading 2 says what follows. The paper's own limitation is of that kind: a distractor and static content of one semantic class, close together, are not told apart (row 796). It could absorb the person's part only by removing the person | A feature map per training image, computed once before training. A supplied mask per image would replace network and classifier, which turns the technique into masks left out of E7 | RobustNeRF's four scenes and NeRF On-the-go, PSNR, SSIM and LPIPS against clean test images: SLS-mlp 22.69 to 35.85 dB against 3DGS's 21.68 to 31.80, and close to 3DGS trained on the distractor-free images on two scenes (33.60 against 33.69 dB, 25.15 against 25.42). The ablation runs from the plain robust filter through SLS-agg to SLS-mlp (its Figure 9). No experiment has a subject that every image shows differently (row 796) | 30,000 iterations, as 3DGS; SLS-mlp is called marginally slower than the clustering, with no figure (row 796). Here: the feature extraction once per training image, and a classifier of 1 × 1 convolutions per iteration |
| **WildGaussians, the uncertainty** (Kulhanek et al., NeurIPS 2024, arXiv 2407.08447; a search seed): a per-pixel mask from an uncertainty predicted on DINOv2 features, trained on the DINO-feature similarity of render and image | DINOv2 ViT-S/14: **pass**, its publisher states Apache-2.0 for code and weights (row 367; this section's estimators table). D-89's `dinov2_s14` loads the same weights | MIT for the authors' code, at commit 66fa22a; the renderer it uses is under the Gaussian-Splatting License, for research and evaluation only (row 797). Mixed: only the license file was opened, and the method is the paper's | A loss on today's primitives, with reading 2's change to `total_loss`. It adds a trainable affine map, the uncertainty predictor, with a loss and an optimizer group of its own, and a DINOv2 pass on each render. With the field off, untouched | Nothing new: the rendered image (section 2.3) | The primitives are untouched. The paper found that weighting the loss by the uncertainty made densification unstable, because densification reads the gradient magnitudes the weight scales; it therefore turns the uncertainty into a binary mask, so that no gradient is scaled up (row 797). E10's g is read the same way (reading 2) | It assumes occluders that are in some images and not in others, over a static scene. The uncertainty is a function of the training image's features alone, so it marks kinds of content wherever they appear. Its signal, the DINO-feature disagreement of render and image, tolerates the appearance changes that MSE and DSSIM residuals mistake for occluders, and needs no trusted camera. On private scene A the content that disagrees is the person, in every image: a predictor on features would mark the person and mask it, as SpotLessSplats' row says (reading 2). The paper's own failure is of that kind: water that is not multi-view consistent is taken for a transient object, and the uncertainty does not help on that scene (row 797) | DINOv2 features of each training image, precomputable; the paper resizes images to at most 350 px for them (row 797). A weight map or a mask per image, supplied as files, would replace predictor and network | NeRF On-the-go and Photo Tourism. Table 3 isolates it: without the uncertainty 20.27 against 23.03 dB at high occlusion and 24.32 against 24.63 dB on Photo Tourism, and no gain at low occlusion (row 797) | 0.50 GPU hours against 3DGS's 0.35 on an RTX 4090, for the whole method (row 797). Here: a ViT-S/14 forward on each render per iteration, which `perc_net` `dinov2_s14` already costs at half resolution (D-89) |
| **WildGaussians, the appearance model** (the same paper): an embedding per image and one per Gaussian, mapped by an MLP to an affine colour transform per Gaussian | none: no estimator | As the row above (row 797) | Nearest class: a change to a quantity or to motion, since it adds a trainable embedding per primitive, a tenth tensor beside §1.1's nine, and makes a primitive's colour depend on the training image. The variant with the image embedding alone, which the paper's ablation finds too weak for local changes, adds nothing per primitive. For one fixed appearance the transform can be baked back into plain SH, so `ftgs-if` need not change (row 797). With the field off, untouched | It changes the colours that enter rasterization, E2's output, and renders twice per iteration, with and without the transform; both backends take a colour per primitive already (section 2.3) | An embedding per primitive has to follow clone, split (E17) and relocation (E11) as the nine tensors do, with its Adam moments (§1.3 Growth). Constant orientation and scale and E1 are untouched. An embedding per image is 1,080 embeddings on private scene A; per camera, 9 | It assumes images that differ in appearance (exposure, illumination, day and night) and agree in geometry: it absorbs colour only, the part nobody has measured on private scene A. Only the license file was opened | Nearest class: a loss on today's primitives, since the primitives, the render and E7 are unchanged. Read, not run: U-12 compares no camera gradient | The primitives are untouched. The first model would have to be one that cannot fit each camera separately (this ticket's reading) | It absorbs misalignment whatever its cause, by moving each image's pixels to where a consensus model renders them: no view is trusted above the model.
